## [Simple Features Outperform Most LLM Monitors on AI Control Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41713) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.41713) shows that simple code-style and problem-level features, without executing any code, outperform 20 of 24 open-weight LLM monitors on a standard AI control benchmark for detecting backdoored code. The authors also identify label leakage in the dataset—honest solutions were written by humans and backdoors by older language models—and find that one in seven backdoors fails validity checks. This finding challenges the validity of a widely used AI control evaluation, suggesting that high monitor scores may reflect superficial author-contrast cues rather than genuine sabotage detection. It has important implications for AI safety evaluation methodology and for how backdoor detection benchmarks should be constructed. Swapping one class at a time for Claude Sonnet 5 code shows the features reward author contrast: human code versus Sonnet 5 backdoors separates at 0.95 versus 0.79 for original pairs. A Sonnet 5 monitor appears to catch 77% of older models' backdoors but only 37% of Sonnet 5's, rising to 78% versus 61% after validity verification; the authors release a rebuilt dataset and checks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: AI control research assumes a monitor can catch a capable model that secretly sabotages its work, often tested by having models insert backdoors into code. In such benchmarks, honest and backdoored solutions are labeled, and monitors are scored on their ability to distinguish them. Data leakage occurs when information unavailable at prediction time—such as authorship style—inadvertently reveals labels, inflating performance.

## [Latent-Lens bridges frozen VLM and SLM via latent-space codec](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41751) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Latent-Lens introduces a lightweight codec that enables a frozen vision-language model (VLM) to communicate visual information directly to a frozen small language model (SLM) through hidden states, avoiding lossy discrete text. The codec trains only ~1.6M parameters (0.2% of combined model size) and achieves 81.0% on CLEVR and 65.4% on GQA, compared to 58.3% and 61.8% for text-based communication with the same LoRA budget. This work addresses a real bottleneck in modular multi-agent systems where SLMs are forced to rely on lossy text descriptions of visual content, and demonstrates that latent communication can significantly boost performance for capacity-limited models. It opens a path toward more efficient and accurate collaboration between independently pretrained vision and language models without fine-tuning their weights. The codec consists of a domain encoder that projects VLM hidden states into the SLM's embedding space and an attention decoder formalized as a LoRA that teaches the SLM to attend to visual prefixes. It was evaluated with TinyLlama-1.1B and InternVL2-1B, which have different architectures, vocabularies, and hidden dimensions, and on image captioning (Flickr8k) it improves over the text baseline by +16.5 points.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) combine visual perception with language understanding, while small language models (SLMs) are compact text-only models with limited capacity. In modular multi-agent systems, these models typically exchange information as discrete text, which loses spatial and fine-grained visual details. Latent communication instead passes internal hidden states between models, and LoRA is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that freezes pretrained weights and injects small trainable matrices.

## [Paper disentangles implicit task assumptions in continual learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41792) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.41792) separates implicit assumptions about tasks in continual learning into data, partition, and learner levels, and shows through controlled teacher-student experiments that these levels do not necessarily coincide. The experiments demonstrate that partitioning the same underlying trajectory at different granularities, changing presentation order, and varying similarity structure all lead to different measured outcomes. This conceptual clarification could influence how continual learning benchmarks are designed and interpreted, since reported quantities often implicitly refer to different levels. It affects researchers and practitioners who compare methods or report metrics, as inconsistent assumptions may lead to misleading conclusions. The paper reviews representative benchmarks to determine which properties are explicitly stated, and notes that when underlying data cannot be inspected (e.g., foundation models with undisclosed pre-training corpora), its properties are inherited by the model's initial state. The analysis is supported by controlled teacher-student experiments rather than large-scale empirical validation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Continual learning studies how models accumulate knowledge from data received over time, typically by dividing a dataset into tasks presented sequentially. Key challenges include catastrophic forgetting, where neural networks rapidly forget previously learned information when learning new tasks. Benchmarks often vary in how tasks are defined and partitioned, but the assumptions behind these choices are rarely made explicit.

## [Observable-Specific Validity Tests for Scientific AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41939) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces observable-specific validity records for scientific AI that link a reduction to its reference model, error measure, tolerance, parameter domain, and interventions. It demonstrates that imposing dimensional homogeneity reduces energy scale transfer error from 52.3% to 0.0768% and raises the median fraction jointly satisfying gap and response tolerances from zero to 100% across 16 repeated neural fits. This work addresses a critical gap in validating scientific AI by providing independently checkable certificates that go beyond simple error tolerances, ensuring models capture physical responses and transfer correctly. It could impact how AI models are certified in physics and chemistry, where reproducing a quantity like energy is insufficient without matching its response to interventions. The framework is demonstrated on a singularly perturbed oscillator with explicit displacement and velocity certificates recovering distinct convergence orders, and on exact Hubbard dimer relations resolving coupling domains. For two mechanisms observed with Gaussian noise whose responses first differ at order x^k, the authors derive an exact fixed control query requirement scaling as x^{-2k} and verify its full success curve numerically, alongside a 256-task evaluation interface.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Scientific AI often approximates complex physical systems, but an approximation can reproduce a quantity like energy while missing its response to changes, or satisfy an error tolerance without being certified by a bound. Dimensional homogeneity is a basic plausibility check from dimensional analysis ensuring equations are consistent in units. The Hubbard dimer is a simple two-site quantum model used to study electron interactions, and singularly perturbed oscillators are systems with a small parameter causing multiple time scales.

## [Aligning MLLMs with Human Cognition via Semantic Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41951) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes a framework that aligns Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) with human similarity judgments by learning dimension-grounded rationales derived from 66 interpretable semantic dimensions based on 4.70 million Odd-One-Out judgments. The method improves model consistency with human similarity judgments and increases representational alignment with human fMRI data while retaining competitive performance on standard multimodal benchmarks. This work offers a data-driven route to incorporate human cognitive structure into MLLM alignment, potentially improving interpretability and human-AI alignment in multimodal reasoning tasks. It bridges cognitive science and AI, showing that behavior-derived semantic dimensions can guide models to make decisions more like humans. The framework infers the most salient decision dimension for each triplet from Odd-One-Out judgments and converts it into a linguistic rationale, enabling MLLMs to learn both the choice and a justification. Validation includes searchlight Representational Similarity Analysis (RSA) on the fMRI Natural Scenes Dataset (NSD), showing increased model-human representational alignment; code is available on GitHub.

## [LLM-SR-UQ Quantifies Structural Uncertainty in LLM-Guided Symbolic Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42024) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces LLM-SR-UQ, a framework that retains competing equation structures from LLM-guided symbolic regression and uses their disagreement to widen prediction intervals under extrapolation. Experiments on the LLM-SRBench dataset show that standard conformal intervals achieve nominal in-distribution coverage but often fail under extrapolation, while LLM-SR-UQ substantially improves coverage attainment. This work addresses a critical gap in uncertainty quantification for AI-driven scientific discovery, where distinct equations can fit observed data similarly but yield vastly different extrapolations. Improved uncertainty estimates could make LLM-guided symbolic regression more trustworthy for high-stakes applications in physics, biology, and engineering. The framework extends standard fixed-width conformal prediction to quantify structural intervals that measure disagreement among candidate equations and identify where prediction intervals should widen. It preserves in-distribution coverage and shows good generalizability when applied to other symbolic-regression methods.

**Background**: Symbolic regression is a type of regression analysis that searches the space of mathematical expressions to find the model that best fits a dataset, often using genetic programming or neural networks. Conformal prediction is an uncertainty quantification method that produces statistically valid prediction intervals under the assumption of exchangeability. LLM-SRBench is a benchmark with 239 challenging problems across four scientific domains designed to evaluate LLM-based symbolic regression.

## [Loops Added After Pretraining Only Pay Off for Small Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42030) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper empirically tests whether adding loops to already-trained models justifies the extra inference compute. Across Qwen3 and Qwen3.5 models from 0.8B to 9B parameters, only the looped 0.8B model beats both an equal-parameter baseline (the same model trained without loops) and an equal-FLOP baseline (the best unlooped use of the same inference compute). This challenges the popular assumption that looping a trained model can buy extra compute without adding parameters, showing the benefit is limited to small models under a fixed parameter budget. It offers a useful, somewhat counterintuitive data point for the machine-learning efficiency community when deciding whether to add loops post-pretraining. Re-applying m of N layers makes each token about (N+m)/N times as expensive, so the loop directly increases inference cost. The result is not due to easy benchmarks: on the same benchmarks the unlooped 9B model still scores up to 13 points higher than the 4B model, showing ample room for improvement.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Looping is a parameter-efficient architecture that re-applies a block of layers so each token passes through the block more than once, mimicking the depth of deeper networks without increasing parameter count, disk size, memory footprint, or training cost. Loops learned during large-scale pretraining can justify their inference cost, but whether loops added after pretraining pay off is unclear. FLOPs (floating-point operations) are a common measure of inference compute, and comparing at equal FLOPs ensures a fair test against simply using the same compute without loops.

## [Learning Clean Generative Models from Noisy Data via Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42036) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes a method to learn a generative model of clean images using only noisy observations by combining Noise2Noise regression with a consistency condition. It derives conditions under which consistency uniquely determines the continuation to the clean image distribution and validates the approach on CIFAR-10, photon-counting microscopy, and synthetic aperture radar data. This work enables training diffusion-like generative models without access to clean ground-truth images, which is valuable in domains like microscopy and radar where clean data is hard to obtain. It could broaden the applicability of generative modeling to scientific imaging and other noisy-data scenarios. Noise2Noise regression alone can only learn posterior predictions at or above the training noise level, so the consistency condition is imposed to repair predictions below that level. The conditions are verified for Poisson, binomial, Gamma, and negative-binomial noise, and experiments show that without consistency the continuation beyond the training noise level severely deteriorates.

**Background**: Diffusion models generate images by gradually denoising from pure noise, requiring posterior predictions at every noise level. Noise2Noise is a technique that trains denoisers using only pairs of noisy observations, without clean targets. Synthetic aperture radar (SAR) is a radar imaging method that produces images from reflected pulses and is inherently noisy.

The paper proves a lower complexity bound of Ω(n + n^{5/7} ε^{-2/7}) for second-order convex finite-sum optimization under individual smoothness and designs a Taylor-corrected lazy Newton method achieving a matching near-optimal upper bound of Õ(n + n^{5/7} ε^{-2/7}). This work shows that individual smoothness enables provable acceleration over averaged smoothness, improving the exponent from 6/7 to 5/7 and advancing the theoretical understanding of second-order methods for finite-sum problems. The algorithm uses a novel second-order self-bounding inequality and optimal Monteiro-Svaiter acceleration, matching the lower bound up to logarithmic factors; the improvement relies on the stronger assumption of individual smoothness and convexity.

## [CRoS: Scrambled CoT Causality Reward Boosts Accuracy and Faithfulness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42054) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces CRoS (Causality Reward on Scrambled CoTs), a new chain-of-thought causality metric that perturbs the order of reasoning steps without altering their content, thereby avoiding the confounding effects of information changes in existing perturbation-based metrics. On Reasoning Gym with Gemma-3-4B, CRoS achieves 46.8% out-of-domain accuracy on five unseen logic and games datasets, outperforming the strongest baseline (43.8%), and improves CoT faithfulness with a hint acknowledgment score of 34.0% versus 20.2%. This work addresses a real flaw in RLVR causality metrics—perturbation-induced confounding—and shows that a better causality reward can improve both out-of-domain accuracy and reasoning faithfulness, which is important for the active LLM reasoning and RLVR research area. If validated, such rewards could lead to more reliable and interpretable reasoning models. CRoS is evaluated on Reasoning Gym with Gemma-3-4B, where it also improves CoT quality, consistently outperforming baselines in CoT-Pass@K from K=1 to 128 (averaging 43.4% vs. 41.4%). The method is a single arXiv preprint (arXiv:2610.42054) and has not yet undergone peer review or community discussion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is a post-training method that fine-tunes language models using automatic, rule-based checkers as rewards, and it has become standard for training reasoning models. However, RLVR typically rewards only outcome correctness, which does not guarantee that the chain-of-thought (CoT) causally contributes to the final answer. Existing causality metrics perturb the CoT (e.g., by deleting steps or injecting errors) and measure changes in the answer, but such perturbations also alter information, creating confounding. CRoS instead scrambles the order of steps to preserve information while testing causal dependence.

## [ID-WM: Hierarchical World Model Decouples Subgoal Sampling from Action Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42146) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers from a team including Yann LeCun, Lerrel Pinto, and Mahmoud Assran introduced ID-WM (Implicit Dynamics World Models), a hierarchical world model that separates high-level reachable subgoal sampling across the state manifold from low-level inverse dynamics action grounding. The method reports a 38.5% improvement over state-of-the-art policy and diffusion-based planners across three navigation and manipulation suites, and a 135% gain over single-level world model MPC on complex, unseen long-horizon configurations. Long-horizon planning with world models is a central bottleneck for embodied agents, where cumulative errors and search complexity degrade performance. By prioritizing local physical feasibility over global behavioral patterns, ID-WM offers a principled way to synthesize novel long-horizon plans from short segments, potentially improving test-time adaptation and generalization in robotics and reinforcement learning. The high-level model samples feasible transitions without being constrained by low-level execution details, while the low-level world model acts as an inverse dynamics solver to ground those transitions into precise actions. The approach is evaluated on three navigation and manipulation suites, with videos available at https://id-wm.github.io, though the abstract does not specify real-robot deployment or sim-to-real transfer results.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: World models are learned simulators that let embodied agents predict the consequences of actions and plan at test time, often generalizing better than direct policy learning. Hierarchical approaches tackle long-horizon tasks by decomposing them into subgoals or skills, but rigid macro-actions or latent skills can yield brittle representations and limited fine-grained control. Inverse dynamics models map desired state transitions back to actions, and are increasingly used in robotics to ground abstract plans into executable control.

## [Shared Modular Recurrence Transformer Improves Universal Morphology Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08630) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces a transformer-based architecture with shared modular recurrence for multi-robot control, which recovers partially available contextual features through modular interactions. Evaluated on a large set of MuJoCo robots, it shows substantial improvement in zero-shot generalization to unseen dynamics, kinematics, and topologies across four environments. A universal controller that generalizes to new robot morphologies could greatly improve computational and data efficiency in multi-robot reinforcement learning, reducing the need to train separate policies for each robot. This work advances toward that goal by handling highly dissimilar morphologies and unseen contexts. The method extends MetaMorph and ModuMorph with shared modular recurrence, yielding Recurrent MetaMorph (R-MeMo) and Recurrent ModuMorph (R-MoMo). It assumes contextual features are only partially available and leverages modular interactions to recover them, enabling better generalization.

**Background**: Contextual Markov Decision Processes (MDPs) are a framework where the dynamics and rewards depend on a context, allowing agents to adapt to different environments. In universal morphology control, the goal is to design a single controller that works across many robot designs, often by exploiting modular structure. Transformers have been used to process contextual information and modular architectures, but generalizing to unseen morphologies remains challenging.

<a id="item-68"></a>
## [ThinkSafe: Self-Generated Safety Alignment for Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.23143) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ThinkSafe proposes a self-generated safety alignment method for reasoning models that steers refusals from the frozen student and filters responses with a safety guard, avoiding external teacher distillation. It formalizes safety realignment as a KL projection and shows on DeepSeek-R1-Distill and Qwen3 that it improves safety while maintaining competitive reasoning performance. This work addresses the safety degradation that reasoning models can suffer after reinforcement learning, offering a way to restore safety without the reasoning performance loss often caused by teacher distillation. It is relevant to AI safety and alignment researchers, and the release of code, models, and datasets supports reproducibility and further research. The KL-projection analysis shows that the student's native filter-accepted distribution is uniquely KL-optimal, and it provides a bound on steering-induced target distortion plus a sufficient condition for lower average target KL than teacher distillation. On Qwen3-0.6B, ThinkSafe achieves better safety and comparable reasoning to GRPO in about one-seventh of the wall-clock time, including data generation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Large reasoning models are often trained with reinforcement learning to improve their reasoning abilities, but this can degrade their safety behavior. Safety alignment aims to make models refuse harmful requests, and one common approach is distillation from an external teacher model, which can restore safety but may hurt reasoning. KL divergence is a measure of how one probability distribution differs from another, and KL projection refers to finding the closest distribution to a target under this measure. Refusal steering is a technique that adjusts model activations to induce safe refusals.

<a id="item-69"></a>
## [Causal Inference Exposes Look-Ahead Bias in LLM User Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11519) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper formalizes controllable user simulation as a causal inference problem and shows that standard supervised fine-tuning of LLM simulators on post-hoc trajectory labels introduces a look-ahead bias. This bias breaks causal consistency and causes evaluation variance to explode geometrically under policy shift, a phenomenon the authors term controllability collapse. This work bridges causal inference and LLM-based user simulation, revealing a structural flaw in common evaluation practices for conversational AI. It offers theoretical conditions and practical training mitigations that could lead to more reliable counterfactual evaluation of dialogue systems. The authors propose three training-time interventions—a priori controls, step-wise dynamic controls, and direct policy-conditioned learning—to restore causal consistency. Empirical results show that standard global controls distort conversational distributions and collapse behavioral diversity, while their causally grounded simulators eliminate look-ahead bias, preserve natural variance, and generalize zero-shot to unseen agent behaviors.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Off-policy evaluation (OPE) in reinforcement learning estimates the value of a target policy using data collected by a different behavior policy. In conversational AI, offline datasets often fail to cover rare scenarios or test new policies, motivating controllable user simulators built by prompting or fine-tuning LLMs. Look-ahead bias, common in causal inference, occurs when future information leaks into training labels, breaking causal consistency.

## [Teaching LLMs When Not to Know: Temporal Critique Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14636) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2605.14636v2) systematically analyzes temporal leakage in LLMs, showing that prompting can steer models into a temporal frame but cannot verify temporal admissibility, and proposes TCFT, a Temporal Critique Fine-Tuning framework that trains models to identify post-cutoff leakage and judge temporal admissibility. Experiments with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct show TCFT reduces average leakage by 41.89 and 37.79 percentage points, respectively, outperforming prompting and SFT baselines. Temporal leakage is a critical failure mode for LLMs used in forecasting, historical analysis, and any scenario requiring reasoning from a past standpoint, as models may inadvertently exploit future knowledge. This work highlights that supervised fine-tuning alone is insufficient because ex-ante correctness is relational, and offers a targeted fine-tuning approach that could improve reliability in time-sensitive applications. The study finds that explicit cutoff statements outperform implicit historical framings, and prefix constraints reduce leakage more effectively than suffix constraints. TCFT trains models on a triple of query, cutoff, and candidate response to identify leakage, explain boundary violations, and judge admissibility, with leakage reductions measured on Qwen2.5 models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Ex-ante reasoning requires a model to answer using only information available before a specified cutoff date, without relying on knowledge of later events. Temporal leakage occurs when a model inadvertently uses future information, undermining the validity of its reasoning. Prior work such as the ExAnte benchmark has quantified this leakage, but effective mitigation remains an open challenge.

## [GENSTRAT: Procedurally Generated Games for LLM Strategic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23238) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GENSTRAT introduces a system that procedurally generates two-player zero-sum imperfect-information betting games to evaluate LLM strategic reasoning, creating a 50-game benchmark and running a tournament of 12 models across over 40,000 matches. This approach addresses benchmark saturation and contamination in fixed game suites, providing a scalable and fresh evaluation method that could become a standard for assessing LLM strategic capabilities. Each game is characterized along five complexity axes (state space, temporal contingency, opponent dependence, private information, precision) and measured via Monte Carlo simulation; on the eight hardest games, the performance gap between best and worst models is 1.7 times larger than across the full set.

Procedural generation creates novel game instances on demand, ensuring that models cannot memorize solutions. Two-player zero-sum imperfect-information games are a classic testbed for strategic reasoning in game theory and AI, requiring players to make decisions without full knowledge of the opponent's state.

## [Causal Graph and Attention Mechanism Improve RAG Retrieval Precision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21702) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.21702v2) reframes the terminal retrieval failure in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) as a causal inference problem, modeling shared keywords between query and document as a latent common cause and proposing a training-free attention-style re-scoring rule. The method promotes a relevant guideline from rank 6 to top 3 on a 471-document enterprise knowledge base and improves mean target rank from 2.88 to 1.25 on a diagnostic corpus, while a trained cross-encoder reranker barely helps (2.63). This work addresses a growing real-world problem: as proprietary knowledge bases expand, dense-vector similarity increasingly returns keyword-matching but information-poor documents, and the proposed training-free fix can be deployed locally without model retraining. It also delineates a clear applicability boundary, showing the method complements rather than replaces neural rerankers, which matters for practitioners building production RAG pipelines. The causal graph is grounded in Reichenbach's common cause principle: shared keywords form latent common cause A, residual keywords form latent set B, and the retrieved document acts as a collider (A -> d <- B), which licenses re-scoring by cosine similarity between the query embedding and the weighted centroid embedding of B. On three BEIR benchmarks the method underperforms the similarity baseline, but a corpus-level calibration gate selects the correct regime with at least 95% reliability.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) grounds large language model outputs by retrieving external documents at query time, typically using dense-vector similarity optionally followed by a reranker. Reichenbach's common cause principle states that a correlation between two events must arise from one causing the other or from a shared common cause, and in causal graphs a collider is a node with two incoming arrows that can open an associational path. This paper uses these concepts to explain why keyword overlap alone is an unreliable signal of relevance in retrieval.

## [SIRF internalizes platform policies into an 8B model via continued pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11752) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SIRF (Spec-Internalized Risk Foundation Model) internalizes a platform's complex content policies into an 8B model's weights via policy-grounded continued pretraining (CPT), using synthetic policy data generated without extra human annotation through EntiGraph, MAGA rewriting, and account-level chain-of-thought. In a controlled same-source comparison, SIRF-8B-SFT reaches 71.3% Black Recall@P95, a +15.1pp gain over the Qwen3-8B-SFT baseline, using only ~70M CPT tokens. This shows that internalizing policies via CPT can substantially raise the share of risky content auto-handled at high precision and second-level latency, which is the real deployment constraint for industrial risk control. It offers a practical recipe for production moderation systems and suggests smaller specialized models can match or exceed far larger systems under a verdict-only interface. The gain is attributed to policy internalization because the comparison holds policy injection and verdict-only output form constant, differing only in policy-grounded CPT; the ~70M-token CPT does not harm general ability. SIRF is deployed as a tree-model adjudication layer that recovers 20% more mis-penalized samples, and it transfers to a freezing scenario with ~70% relative mis-penalization reduction at low cost.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Industrial content risk control requires high-precision decisions at second-level latency, so the key metric is how much risk can be auto-handled rather than average accuracy. Continued pretraining (CPT) feeds domain text into an existing model to adapt it before fine-tuning, while EntiGraph is a synthetic-data method that generates diverse text from source documents so a model can learn knowledge without access to the originals. SIRF combines these ideas to bake platform policies directly into model weights instead of relying only on prompt injection or supervised fine-tuning.

<a id="item-74"></a>
## [LazyAgent brings demand-driven lazy execution to LLM agent runtimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23058) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LazyAgent is presented as the first complete formulation of demand-driven execution for agent runtimes, running a ready step only when the current request actually needs it and keeping that demanded set exact as the plan changes. Measured against an eager runtime, it saves 51.7% and 52.1% of container time on release gates, 12.8% of tokens on DataSciBench plans at matched quality, 24.0% of dollars in a live LangGraph application, and 42.0% of CPU on production workflows with at most 0.43% overhead. The paper shows that eager agent runtimes waste 62.2% of CPU on work requests never needed across 303 requests on 154 production workflows, so demand-driven execution could substantially cut cost and latency for anyone deploying LLM agents. It also argues that public agent benchmarks are designed like eager runtimes and therefore cannot reveal this inefficiency, calling on industry to build benchmarks that measure execution efficiency. Because demand is defined exactly, LazyAgent can prove where it must save and where it must tie, and it can act as a query optimizer that knows the bill before a plan runs and chooses among candidate workflows. The same demanded set steers physical operations such as projection, which saves 80-89% of a shared CI step where an identical eager control saves 0.0%, and when the LLM re-plans after real code failures it solves every solvable goal with 11.7% fewer tokens per goal.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Agent runtimes that plan before acting typically execute each step as soon as its dependencies are ready, an eager evaluation strategy that mirrors how most programming languages evaluate expressions. Lazy evaluation, by contrast, defers computation until a value is actually needed, and database query optimizers similarly choose among physical execution plans based on estimated cost. LazyAgent combines these two ideas, applying lazy demand analysis and cost-based plan selection to agent-authored programs.

## [Formal Framework Separates Search Gains from Coverage Expansion in Self-Improving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10611) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The arXiv paper 2610.10611v2 introduces a formal framework with explicit support and transcript interfaces to distinguish genuine search improvements from changes in accepted-task coverage in self-improving agents. It validates the framework with an executable Boolean-program repair agent experiment involving 36,864 paired runs, showing that counterexample feedback reduces verifier calls without changing coverage, while an enforced permission change admits a new target with a proof excluding all old-interface programs. This work provides a rigorous way to evaluate self-improving agents, helping researchers and safety practitioners avoid mistaking changes in task coverage for genuine capability gains. It has implications for AI safety and agentic AI evaluation, as it specifies the evidence needed to claim improved search, wider accepted capability, and reliable successor selection. The framework introduces concepts such as native reach (tasks accepted with default support) and closure frontier (tasks accepted with any support the declared interface permits), along with a semantic containment criterion and guard-transfer invariance to prevent representation changes from being counted as capability expansion. The paper also discusses exact and uniformly gapped randomized verification, independent majority amplification, and adaptive-selection contracts for interpreting successor scores as deployment improvement evidence.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Self-improving AI agents are systems that get better over time by using feedback loops, persistent memory, reflection, and self-evaluation. However, a performance score alone does not reveal whether improvement comes from searching longer, receiving additional support, or modifying how outputs are proposed and verified. This paper builds on task-relative verification and bounded verification with hidden terminal randomness to provide a formal account of these distinctions.

## [ReState: State-Driven Iterative Reasoning Framework for MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15620) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes ReState, a unified iterative reasoning framework that reformulates multimodal chain-of-thought from a stage-driven pipeline into a state-driven dynamic process. It introduces Unified Reasoning State Modeling (URSM) and Closed-Loop Reasoning-State Learning (CRSL), and constructs the VersaCoT-10K dataset spanning eight visual reasoning domains. This work addresses fragmented reasoning and the training-inference reasoning-state gap in existing MCoT methods, potentially enabling more coherent and self-refining multimodal reasoning without extra modules like critics or reflection stages. It could influence future MLLM reasoning system designs and benchmarks. URSM unifies reasoning construction, preservation, and correction as state transitions in a shared space, while CRSL uses self-generated historical states during training and recursive state updates during inference to mitigate the state gap. Experiments show adaptability across MLLM backbones and strong performance on public benchmarks, with code and data available at the project page.

## [Audit finds governed confidence score for text-to-SQL poorly separates right from wrong answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17256) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper audits a production confidence-scoring design for text-to-SQL answers on the BIRD dev set (1,534 questions, GPT-5.1 with five samples per question), finding weak separation between correct and incorrect answers (AUROC 0.624) and that 35.7% of correct answers are withheld. A pre-registered confirmation on Spider dev showed that a simplified agreement-based rule improved AUROC from 0.566 to 0.725. This work highlights that confidence scores used to decide whether to show, warn, or withhold answers in conversational analytics can be poorly calibrated, potentially eroding user trust and hiding correct answers. It provides concrete evidence for practitioners building trustworthy text-to-SQL systems and suggests that simpler agreement-based scores may outperform complex governed designs. The designed score's ranking signal comes almost entirely from agreement among samples, but much of it is lost when combined via a minimum with a checklist that zeroes out on any unsupported claim. The frozen rule was not the best agreement-based rule on Spider, and the evaluation harness and prompts were rebuilt after registration; labels are model-judged with only a ten-answer human pilot showing no disagreement.

AUROC (Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic curve) is a common metric for evaluating how well a confidence score ranks correct answers above incorrect ones, with 0.5 indicating random ranking and 1.0 perfect separation. BIRD and Spider are widely used benchmarks for text-to-SQL, with BIRD focusing on large, varied databases and Spider on cross-domain generalization.

## [Terminal-RSI: Recursive Self-Improving Terminal Agents via Self-Rewrite and Policy-Refine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22192) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Terminal-RSI, a training framework that alternates Recursive Self-Rewrite (RSR) of verified trajectories with critic-inheriting Recursive Policy-Refine (RPR), producing a 27B model that scores 19.84% on Terminal-Bench 4.0, 86.52% on Terminal-Bench 2.1, 16.95% on Terminal-Bench 3.0, and 79.00% on Terminal-Bench Hard. On LHTB, process reward rises from 0.21 to 0.37 and strict completion from 0/46 to 5/46, while SWR100 success doubles from 3.00% to 6.00%. This work demonstrates a concrete recursive self-improvement loop for terminal agents, showing that coupling verified trajectory rewriting with critic-inheriting policy refinement can substantially boost benchmark performance and convert partial progress into strict task completion. It offers a reproducible recipe for building more capable autonomous agents that operate in command-line environments, relevant to researchers and practitioners in LLM agent systems and reinforcement learning. The method introduces Turn-Boundary Skip-Observation GAE (TB-GAE) to retain token-specific baselines with decay across interaction turns, Family Dense Reward (FDR) to calibrate semantic progress across task families, and Success-Prioritized Reward (SPR) to separate strict success from bounded partial credit. A five-stage sequence from Qwen3.8-27B base model through RSR-1, RPR-1, RSR-2, and RPR-2 yields the final Terminal-RSI-27B, coordinated by a Warm-up Critic Model, recursive critic handoff, and stage-specific learning rates.

Recursive self-improvement (RSI) refers to a process where an AI system improves its own capabilities, and in LLM agents this often means iteratively refining policies or rewriting successful trajectories. Generalized Advantage Estimation (GAE) is a reinforcement learning technique for estimating advantage functions with reduced variance, commonly used in actor-critic methods.

A new arXiv position paper (2610.24662v2) introduces the concept of a 'population health world model' (PHWM), arguing that digital medicine must move beyond task-specific prediction to represent how interventions and resource allocation reshape future health trajectories and population dynamics. The authors identify three structural gaps—dynamic health-state modeling, population-level intervention intelligence, and validation/equity/governance—and propose three minimal capabilities needed to address them. This framing could shift AI in healthcare from isolated prediction tasks toward decision-support systems that model the consequences of actions, potentially improving resource allocation and care adaptation across heterogeneous populations. It highlights the need for validation and equity governance, which are critical for real-world deployment. The paper is a high-level vision rather than a concrete technical breakthrough, and it emphasizes the importance of social, contextual, and public-health sensing data for action-oriented digital medicine. It calls for representation learning under action-dependent observation, multi-scale intervention intelligence, and validation with explicit decision boundaries.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: World models are AI systems that learn an internal representation of an environment to simulate outcomes and support planning. In digital medicine, most current models are trained for specific prediction tasks (e.g., diagnosis or risk scores) rather than modeling how interventions change future states. Population health focuses on health outcomes across groups and the distribution of resources, and AI fairness research has shown that models can make different recommendations based on socioeconomic and demographic factors.

<a id="item-80"></a>
## [R^3: Asymmetric Memory Boosts Multi-Turn LLM Jailbreak Success](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31937) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces R^3 (Reject, Remember, Retry), a black-box multi-LLM framework where the attacker conditions on a compact state rather than the raw conversation transcript, removing failed turns from the target's context while remembering them along with failure reasons. Across two benchmarks and six target LLMs, R^3 outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by up to 53% in ASR@5 under matched query budgets. This work demonstrates that asymmetric memory—where the attacker retains information the target cannot see—significantly improves multi-turn jailbreak effectiveness, highlighting a critical vulnerability in LLM safety mechanisms. It also shows that a low-capacity attacker with R^3's state can outperform high-capacity attackers without it by up to 34% in ASR@5, suggesting that memory design may matter more than raw model capability for adversarial attacks. Ablations attribute most gains to remembering rejected turns, and the risk score acts as a coarse threshold rather than a progress measure. The framework is black-box and uses multiple LLMs, with the attacker maintaining a compact state that includes failure reasons.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute a harmful objective across multiple dialogue exchanges to evade detection by LLM safety filters, often using gradual prompt escalation as in the Crescendo attack. Attack Success Rate (ASR) is a key metric measuring the percentage of adversarial attempts that successfully induce policy-violating outputs, and ASR@5 specifically measures success within five queries. Existing multi-turn attackers typically condition on the same conversation history as the target, limiting their ability to learn from failures.

## [AgentOrch Benchmarks Global Workflow Orchestration vs. Step-by-Step LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33375) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AgentOrch introduces a benchmark that directly compares global workflow orchestration with step-by-step LLM agent interaction on 650 enterprise SOPs and 3,220 runtime variants, finding that step-by-step remains more accurate for every evaluated model but the accuracy gap has shrunk by about 79% from early models to the strongest recent model. This matters because global orchestration can generate a complete executable workflow in advance without further LLM calls, substantially reducing inference overhead, so narrowing the accuracy gap could enable enterprise agents that deliver higher accuracy with fewer LLM calls. The paper identifies three routes to further narrow the gap: incremental workflow construction improves Global accuracy by an average of 9.98 percentage points without runtime execution feedback, revising complete workflows with execution feedback improves all evaluated models including 6.13 pp on held-out runtime variants, and targeted training on complete workflows improves accuracy by an average of 12.18 pp across all five trained models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Enterprise workflow agents typically rely on step-by-step interaction, where the agent executes an action, observes the result, and invokes an LLM again to decide the next step. Global orchestration instead generates a complete executable workflow in advance and executes it without further LLM calls, reducing inference overhead but remaining less accurate. AgentOrch is a benchmark designed to compare these two approaches under matched tools, environments, and evaluation.

## [LegoSR unifies seven equation solvers with an adaptive agent for scientific discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33465) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LegoSR is a unified framework that integrates seven equation discovery solvers through a common equation representation and evaluation process, and includes an agent that selects solvers and adapts searches based on solver knowledge and past experience. On noisy ordinary and partial differential equation benchmarks, LegoSR Agent recovers nearly as many equations as the union of seven expert-tuned solvers and outperforms a generic agent with the same resources by up to 29 percentage points. This work addresses the fragmentation in equation discovery, where solvers use incompatible interfaces and metrics, by providing a common platform that makes results comparable and enables agent-guided automation. It could accelerate automated scientific discovery by making powerful solvers more accessible and adaptive, potentially benefiting researchers in physics, biology, and engineering who rely on symbolic regression. The framework supports searches constrained by partially known equations, and three case studies of increasing difficulty show the agent's role evolving from simply running a solver to selecting one and then to constructing a partially known equation from failed searches. However, as an arXiv preprint, the work has not yet undergone peer review, and its performance is demonstrated on specific noisy differential equation benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Symbolic regression is a type of regression analysis that searches the space of mathematical expressions to find the model that best fits a dataset, balancing accuracy and simplicity. Equation discovery, also called symbolic regression, aims to learn free-form symbolic equations from experimental data, and is a key task in automated scientific discovery. However, different solvers often use different data interfaces, equation representations, and evaluation criteria, making their results hard to compare. Recent advances in agent-based systems have shown promise in automating scientific discovery by leveraging machine learning and symbolic reasoning.

## [MONOLOGUE: Logic Hijacking Attack Poisons RAG with Single Document](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34829) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose MONOLOGUE, a single-document poisoning framework that avoids explicit refutation by conceding the ground truth and weaving a record-correction narrative to present a counterfactual target as the latest update. Payloads are refined offline via local multi-dimensional gated optimization, and evaluations across three benchmarks, three retrieval backends, and four open-weight generators show the strongest overall attack efficacy among single-document poisoning attacks. This attack exposes a stealthy vulnerability in RAG systems where a single poisoned document can hijack reasoning without triggering guardrails, affecting anyone relying on RAG for trustworthy answers. It highlights the need for stronger corpus provenance controls and defense mechanisms beyond simple contradiction detection. The method uses a grounded record-correction narrative (e.g., dated institutional reattributions) to make the counterfactual seem like a recent update, and refines payloads entirely offline with automated text quality audits and surrogate behavioral verification to avoid suspicious live writes. Evaluations cover three benchmarks, three retrieval backends, and four open-weight generator regimes, though the abstract does not specify exact attack success rates.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) enhances large language models by retrieving relevant documents from an external corpus to ground responses, improving factuality and freshness. However, this creates a corpus-poisoning vulnerability: if an attacker can insert a malicious document into the corpus, the model may retrieve and trust it. Existing single-document attacks often rely on explicit factual refutations or imperative instructions, which modern aligned LLMs may reject or flag via guardrails.

## [Concordat: Typed Sessions Enforce Safety for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37461) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Concordat is a new Python library that models LLM agents as finite-state protocols, enforcing legal tool calls and human-approval gates at runtime. In an evaluation on tau-bench's 50 airline tasks, the standard agent made unauthorized changes in 13 of 50 tasks with GPT-4.1 and 29 of 128 runs with Claude Haiku 5.5, while the Concordat agent made none, with no significant difference in task success. This work addresses a critical safety gap in LLM agents that take consequential actions, such as cancellations or refunds, by moving from prompt-based rules to runtime enforcement. It could influence how developers build trustworthy agents and how benchmarks like tau-bench evaluate safety violations. Concordat checks protocols at definition time and rejects subclasses that could bypass a parent's protocol, a behavioral form of Liskov substitution. It also charges token and tool-call budgets linearly across handoffs, and the authors note that tau-bench's own reward did not detect most violations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Session types and typestate analysis are programming-language techniques that ensure operations occur in a valid order, such as requiring a confirmation before a change. Concordat applies these ideas to LLM agents, which are often given tools that can cancel bookings or issue refunds but are only guided by prompt instructions. The library enforces the protocol at runtime, so the model cannot call tools that are illegal in the current state.

## [Reinforced Adversarial Learning Uses Co-Trained LLM Judge to Replace Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38851) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Reinforced Adversarial Learning (RAL), a post-training method where a co-trained LLM judge converts reference answers alone into the sole reward signal for on-policy training of reasoning LLMs. RAL matches direct RLVR on three verifiable tasks (integer multiplication, code-based arithmetic-expression synthesis, and SMILES-to-formula translation) and outperforms frontier-LLM baselines on scientific abstract generation, including NIH grant abstracts. This work addresses a key limitation of RLVR, which requires explicit correctness signals, by enabling post-training in domains where high-quality answers are recognizable but hard to score. It opens a path toward training reasoning models in scientific and other high-value domains using only reference examples. RAL requires sufficiently capable judges and enough training to match RLVR, and its effectiveness depends on the judge's ability to infer hard-to-specify reward functions from example answers. The method was validated on verifiable tasks using verifiers only for evaluation, and on scientific abstract generation where human scientists and an ensemble of LLM judges found RAL generations harder to distinguish from real abstracts than frontier-LLM baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) is a popular post-training approach for reasoning LLMs, but it relies on explicit, rule-based reward signals that are only available for tasks with checkable answers. LLM-as-a-Judge is a technique where a language model evaluates the quality of another model's output, and co-training such a judge alongside the generator can provide a learned reward. RAL combines these ideas adversarially: the judge learns to distinguish generated answers from reference answers, and its judgments become the reward for the generator.

## [Source-agreement metrics fail when LLMs retell facts as stories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39500) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.39500v2) shows that source-agreement metrics, the standard way to evaluate LLM outputs against a source, measure the wrong construct for fact-to-story reconstruction tasks. Three model families rewrote 2,000 news articles as summaries and as stories, and the authors found that accountability distortion rose up to sevenfold in stories compared to summaries, with two in three determinable cases lightening the perpetrator's responsibility. This matters because source-agreement metrics are widely used to judge LLM factuality, yet they either count intended narrative departures as errors or let real distortions through when withheld. The findings affect AI evaluation, media integrity, and LLM safety, especially as models are increasingly used to turn recorded facts into content for public consumption. The study measured changes across every axis of the five Ws (who, what, when, where, why) and asked whether each detected distortion was warranted by the assigned perspective, setting aside a third of them. Time and manner were replaced rather than lost, place and causation lost their carrying expressions, and the distortions survived claim-level fact checking while leaving the retelling grammatically cleaner than its source; the authors release ReTold-2k, a reconstructable corpus with scoring protocol and per-judge baselines kept unaggregated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Large language models are often evaluated by comparing their output to a source text, using metrics that reward agreement with the source. The five Ws is a journalistic checklist for ensuring a report contains the essential points: who, what, when, where, and why. In reconstruction tasks, however, departing from the source can be the goal, so agreement-based metrics may not capture what actually matters, such as whether responsibility is assigned fairly.

## [EvoTeam: Jointly Evolving Team Organization, Interaction Rules, and Agent Skills for LLM Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39813) ⭐️ 7.0/10

EvoTeam introduces a collaboration-centric evolution framework that jointly evolves team organization, inter-agent interaction rules, and individual agent skills through execution experience. It maintains a library of reusable teams, selects or creates teams for heterogeneous tasks, and guides collaboration with team-specific interaction rules, with experiments across four benchmarks showing effectiveness. This addresses a key gap in LLM-based multi-agent systems: optimizing individual agents in isolation does not guarantee better team collaboration, especially across heterogeneous tasks. By evolving collaboration structures and skills together, EvoTeam could improve adaptability and performance of multi-agent systems in complex, diverse problem-solving scenarios. EvoTeam maintains a library of reusable teams and accumulates collaboration experience within compatible team contexts, while individual skills remain reusable across teams. The approach is evaluated on four benchmarks, and the code is available at https://github.com/DEEP-JLU/EvoTeam.

Existing methods often improve MAS by refining individual agents' prompts, skills, or execution strategies based on task feedback, but such individual improvements may not translate into better team collaboration. Recent work has extended evolution to the collaboration level, adjusting team organization and inter-agent interactions, yet evolving collaboration across heterogeneous tasks remains challenging because prior approaches often constrain reorganization within a shared structure and reuse interaction rules across incompatible team contexts.

## [AnytimeReasoner Optimizes LLM Reasoning Under Token Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13438) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces AnytimeReasoner, a framework that truncates reasoning traces to fit sampled token budgets and uses verifiable dense rewards to train LLMs for anytime reasoning. It also proposes Budget Relative Policy Optimization (BRPO), a variance reduction technique that outperforms GRPO across all thinking budgets in mathematical reasoning tasks. This work addresses the inefficiency of training and deploying LLMs that only optimize final performance under a large fixed token budget, enabling flexible reasoning under varying budget constraints. It could lead to more token-efficient and adaptable reasoning models, benefiting both training costs and inference deployment. The method decouples thinking and summary policies to maximize cumulative reward, and BRPO enhances robustness and efficiency when reinforcing the thinking policy. Empirical results show consistent improvements over GRPO across various prior distributions of token budgets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Scaling test-time compute is crucial for enhancing LLM reasoning, and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards is commonly used to optimize reasoning traces. However, these methods typically assume a large fixed token budget, which is inefficient. Anytime reasoning aims to produce good answers under varying budget constraints, and GRPO is a popular policy optimization algorithm for LLM reasoning.

## [LLINBO: Trustworthy LLM-in-the-Loop Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14756) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LLINBO is a general framework that integrates LLM-based Bayesian optimization with classical BO, using three interaction mechanisms that decide when to act on LLM suggestions and when to revert to classical BO. Each mechanism comes with a no-harm theoretical guarantee ensuring sublinear cumulative regret for common kernels without requiring performance assumptions on the LLM. This work addresses a key limitation of LLM-based BO—the lack of calibrated uncertainty and theoretical guarantees—by providing a principled way to safely leverage LLM suggestions. It enables more reliable and efficient black-box optimization, potentially benefiting researchers and practitioners in AI/ML and optimization communities who use LLMs for expensive experimental design. The framework wraps existing LLM-based BO approaches and provides no-harm guarantees that ensure sublinear cumulative regret for common kernels, while simulation studies show it makes LLM-based BO more reliable and still exploits the LLM's few-shot learning to outperform classical BO. It is also demonstrated on a real-world, expensive 3D printing experiment, and code is available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Bayesian optimization (BO) is a sequential model-based strategy for optimizing expensive black-box functions, using a surrogate model (often a Gaussian process) to predict outcomes and estimate uncertainty. Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently been used to propose high-quality query points by leveraging contextual knowledge, but they lack explicit surrogate modeling and calibrated uncertainty, which can lead to costly unnecessary experiments. LLINBO bridges this gap by integrating LLM suggestions with classical BO in a theoretically sound manner.

## [SOReL and TOReL Enable Fully Offline Reinforcement Learning with Hyperparameter Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.22442) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces SOReL, a fully offline Bayesian model-based reinforcement learning method that learns a posterior over dynamics and uses predictive uncertainty to estimate policy value, enabling hyperparameter selection without any online interactions. It also presents TOReL, a framework that extends this offline tuning approach to arbitrary model-free and model-based offline RL algorithms, with a regret analysis showing minimax-optimal parametric rates under standard regularity conditions. This work addresses a critical limitation of offline RL: existing methods often rely on undocumented online interactions for hyperparameter tuning, undermining the promise of safe and sample-efficient deployment. By enabling fully offline hyperparameter selection with theoretical guarantees, SOReL and TOReL could make offline RL more practical for real-world applications where online exploration is expensive or unsafe, such as healthcare and robotics. SOReL is a Bayesian model-based approach that estimates policy value via predictive uncertainty, and TOReL generalizes the tuning framework to any offline RL algorithm. The regret analysis establishes minimax-optimal parametric rates under standard regularity conditions, though the method's performance may depend on the quality of the Bayesian model and the offline dataset.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Offline reinforcement learning (ORL) aims to learn policies from a fixed dataset of previously collected transitions without any online interaction, promising safe and sample-efficient deployment. Bayesian model-based RL maintains a posterior over environment dynamics to guide decision-making under uncertainty. Hyperparameter tuning is crucial for algorithm performance, but in ORL it is challenging because online evaluation is typically unavailable, and existing methods often resort to online interactions, violating the offline setting. Minimax-optimal parametric rate refers to the best achievable convergence rate for estimating parameters in a statistical model, indicating efficiency.

## [VisualSphinx: Synthetic Visual Logic Puzzles for RL Training of VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23977) ⭐️ 7.0/10

VisualSphinx introduces a large-scale synthetic visual logical reasoning training dataset and a rule-to-image synthesis pipeline that extracts and expands puzzle rules from seed questions to generate executable rendering programs. Using standard GRPO with binary correctness rewards, VLMs trained on VisualSphinx show improved performance on logical reasoning tasks and also benefit other reasoning domains such as algebraic, arithmetic, and geometry reasoning. Current vision-language models lack large-scale, well-structured training data for multimodal logical reasoning, and VisualSphinx addresses this gap with a scalable synthetic data approach. The demonstrated transfer to other reasoning tasks suggests that synthetic visual logic puzzles could be a valuable general-purpose training resource for improving VLM reasoning capabilities. The rule-to-image synthesis pipeline extracts and expands puzzle rules from seed questions and generates executable rendering programs for puzzle assembly, enabling image synthesis with grounded answers. Experiments use standard GRPO with binary correctness rewards as a validation recipe, and the dataset's logical coherence and readability are credited for the performance gains.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) are AI systems that process both images and text to perform multimodal reasoning, which is important for tasks like diagram understanding and spatial problem solving. GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a reinforcement learning algorithm that generates a group of answers per prompt and rewards answers that beat the group average, and it has been used successfully for reasoning-intensive tasks. However, VLMs still lack large-scale, well-structured datasets specifically designed for visual logical reasoning, which VisualSphinx aims to provide.

## [MLLMEraser: Training-Free Test-Time Unlearning for Multimodal LLMs via Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.04217) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MLLMEraser introduces a training-free, test-time unlearning framework for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that uses activation steering to dynamically erase knowledge without parameter updates. It constructs a multimodal erasure direction by contrasting adversarially perturbed knowledge-recall image-text pairs with knowledge-erasure counterparts, and employs an input-aware steering mechanism to adaptively apply the direction only when needed. This approach addresses the computational expense, irreversibility, and knowledge distortion of existing training-based unlearning methods, offering a more efficient and reversible way to remove private data, outdated knowledge, or harmful content from MLLMs. It could enable practical compliance with privacy regulations and dynamic content moderation in deployed vision-language systems. Experiments on LLaVA-1.5 and Qwen-2.5-VL show that MLLMEraser outperforms state-of-the-art MLLM unlearning baselines, achieving stronger forgetting with lower computational cost and minimal utility degradation. The input-aware mechanism preserves performance on retained knowledge while enforcing forgetting on designated content.

**Background**: Machine unlearning aims to remove specific information from trained models, often motivated by privacy regulations like the 'right to be forgotten'. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) combine vision and language understanding, and existing unlearning methods for them typically require retraining via gradient ascent or preference optimization, which is costly and can degrade overall model performance. Activation steering is a technique that modifies a model's internal activations at inference time using steering vectors, allowing control over model behavior without retraining.

## [Visual Thoughts in Multimodal Chain-of-Thought Are Often Unfaithful, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.23482) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2510.23482v2) shows that the visual information in vision-text multimodal chain-of-thought (MCoT) traces generated by large vision-language models after reinforcement fine-tuning is often inaccurate and largely ignored by the model. The authors propose an intervention-based measurement method and a Sufficient-Component Cause Model (SCCM) learning approach to enhance visual faithfulness. This work exposes a critical flaw in current multimodal reasoning: models can produce correct answers while ignoring visual evidence, undermining trust in MCoT as an interpretable reasoning process. The findings could influence future reward design and training methods for multimodal chain-of-thought, impacting researchers and practitioners building reliable vision-language systems. The proposed SCCM is annotation-free and plug-and-play, compatible with various reinforcement fine-tuning methods for MCoT, and empirical results show it substantially improves visual faithfulness across fine-grained perception and reasoning benchmarks. The code is available at https://github.com/EugeneLiu01/Faithful_Thinking_with_Image.

**Background**: Large vision-language models (LVLMs) can be fine-tuned with reinforcement learning to generate interleaved vision-text chain-of-thought traces, where the model produces visual cues (e.g., cropped image regions) alongside textual reasoning steps. Faithfulness here means that the reasoning trace actually reflects the model's decision process—if the visual information is corrupted, the answer should change. Prior work has mainly focused on perceptual hallucinations, but reasoning-level unfaithfulness in multimodal CoT remains underexplored.

Speech-DRAME introduces a bilingual, human-annotated benchmark (Speech-DRAME-EvalBench) with contrastive mismatch conditions and a fine-tuned audio-language evaluator (DRAME-Eval) that achieves pooled Pearson correlations of 0.629 for archetype evaluation and 0.602 for realism evaluation on benchmark-distribution data, significantly outperforming zero- and few-shot audio-language judges. This work addresses a critical gap in speech evaluation by moving beyond coarse scores to assess prosody, emotion, and contextual fit in role-play, providing a resource that could standardize and improve how generative speech models are evaluated for expressive, human-like acting. The benchmark includes a held-out stress test of authentic human acting, where DRAME-Eval's correlation drops to -0.142 (within-speaker +0.029), revealing a substantial synthetic-to-authentic transfer gap compared to human single-rater agreement of +0.34; modality controls confirm judgments depend on both speech and textual context.

Audio-language models (ALMs) combine audio encoders with large language models to process speech and text, and recent work has explored using them as judges for speech evaluation. ## [Scalable Causal Mediation Framework for Root Cause Analysis in Complex Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.14764) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes a scalable causal mediation analysis framework that decomposes total effects into direct and indirect components for large directed acyclic graphs with multiple treatments and mediators. It demonstrates practical utility through applied case studies in fulfillment center logistics, where complex dependencies and non-controllable factors obscure root causes. Traditional mediation analysis fails to scale to high-dimensional DAGs, so this framework addresses a significant gap in root cause analysis for complex operational systems like logistics and cloud infrastructure. It could help practitioners identify mediated pathways and improve intervention strategies in industrial IoT and other interdependent processes. The approach systematically decomposes total effects into interpretable direct and indirect components, but as an arXiv preprint it has not undergone peer review and lacks community discussion. The case study focuses on fulfillment center logistics, and the framework's scalability to other domains like cloud infrastructure is suggested but not fully validated.

Directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) are graphical tools used to represent assumed causal relationships among variables, where arrows indicate causal direction and no cycles are allowed. Root cause analysis aims to identify the underlying causes of failures or anomalies in complex systems, and causal inference methods are increasingly used to go beyond correlation.

## [TIRx: A Unified Tile Primitive ML Compiler for Heterogeneous Backends](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.19092) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TIRx is a new ML compiler that introduces an explicit execution-scope abstraction, tensor layouts with shard/replica/offset semantics, and tile-primitive dispatch to generate efficient kernels across GPUs and AI accelerators. It preserves native-level control while automating recurring address calculation, operand preparation, and instruction-loop generation. This work from Tianqi Chen's group could simplify kernel development across diverse hardware by providing a unified abstraction that maintains peak performance, potentially reducing the need for backend-specific low-level code. It addresses the growing challenge of heterogeneous backends in ML systems. The compiler uses unresolved TilePrimitiveCall IR nodes that carry primitive type, execution scope, operand layouts, target backend, and optional hints, and evaluation shows vendor-level performance on latest GPUs and AI accelerators with substantial reductions in low-level implementation code. It is part of the Apache TVM ecosystem, with TIRx 0.25.0 released.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: ML compilers like TVM and MLIR translate high-level tensor operations into optimized code for specific hardware. As ML kernels evolve rapidly with new optimizations and hardware features, compilers must handle richer memory hierarchies, more forms of thread cooperation, and increasingly heterogeneous backends while retaining control for peak performance. TIRx builds on the TVM ecosystem to provide explicit interfaces for these ingredients.

## [Causal study finds political bias doesn't predict AI disinformation detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22871) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces JudgeGPT and RogueGPT, a dual-axis framework that uses Structural Causal Models to analyze human susceptibility to foundation model hallucinations and disinformation. Analyzing 918 evaluations across five foundation models including GPT-4 and Llama-2, the study finds that political orientation has a negligible association with detection performance (r = -0.10), while 'fake news familiarity' emerges as a candidate mediator (r = 0.35). The findings challenge partisan narratives that political orientation drives susceptibility to disinformation, suggesting instead that pre-bunking interventions should target cognitive source monitoring rather than demographic segmentation. This has implications for designing more effective media literacy programs and trustworthy AI systems in the fight against AI-generated disinformation. The study identifies a 'fluency trap' where GPT-4 outputs (HumanMachineScore: 0.20) bypass Source Monitoring mechanisms and become indistinguishable from human text, and it uses Structural Causal Models to formulate testable causal hypotheses about detection accuracy. As an arXiv preprint, the work has not yet undergone peer review and is based on a limited sample of 918 evaluations.

'Source monitoring' refers to the cognitive process by which humans track the origin of information, and 'pre-bunking' is a technique that preemptively exposes people to weakened forms of misinformation to build resistance.

## [Recovery-based Shielding with Gaussian Processes for Safe RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.12444) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a recovery-based shielding framework that integrates a backup policy (shield) with an RL agent, using Gaussian process (GP) uncertainty quantification to predict safety violations and recover only when necessary, achieving a provable safety lower bound for unknown nonlinear continuous dynamical systems. This work addresses the critical need for provable safety guarantees in safety-critical RL applications, enabling unrestricted exploration and sample-efficient learning without compromising safety, which could impact domains like robotics and autonomous control. The approach uses experience from the shielded agent to construct GP models and performs policy optimization via internal model-based sampling; empirical results show strong performance and strict safety compliance on continuous control benchmarks, though the method assumes a known backup policy and may face scalability challenges with high-dimensional systems.

## [CALMs: Interpretable Models with Local Additivity and Conditional Feature Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16503) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Conditionally Additive Local Models (CALMs), a new model class that maintains local additivity while capturing feature interactions through region-specific univariate shape functions. It also proposes a distillation-based training pipeline that identifies homogeneous regions and fits interpretable shape functions via region-aware backfitting. CALMs offer a better trade-off between interpretability and accuracy than GAMs and GA2Ms, potentially making them attractive for domains like healthcare and finance where both model transparency and predictive performance are crucial. This could advance the adoption of interpretable machine learning in high-stakes decision-making. CALMs allow multiple univariate shape functions per feature, each active in different regions defined by simple logical conditions (thresholds) on interacting features, and experiments show they consistently outperform GAMs and achieve accuracy broadly comparable to GA2Ms while preserving univariate auditability.

**Background**: Generalized additive models (GAMs) are interpretable because they model the response as a sum of univariate functions, but they underfit when feature interactions exist. Model distillation transfers knowledge from a complex model to a simpler one, often to improve interpretability or efficiency.

Place-it-R1 is an end-to-end framework that uses environment-aware MLLM reasoning to guide video diffusion for physically plausible object insertion, and introduces Spatial Direct Preference Optimization (Spatial DPO) to localize physical-violation penalties to the inserted-object region. This work addresses a key limitation of existing diffusion-based video editing methods that produce visually plausible but physically inconsistent results, and could advance the reliability of AI-assisted video editing for content creators and VFX pipelines. The MLLM analyzes the target environment and outputs two forms of guidance: semantic guidance describing the intended physical interaction and spatial guidance providing a valid insertion region per frame. ## [Open Biomedical Knowledge Graphs Built on Samyama Graph Database](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15080) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper presents an open, reproducible biomedical knowledge graph ecosystem built on Samyama, a Rust-native graph database, detailing three foundational graphs (Pathways, Clinical Trials, Drug Interactions) and their expansion into a 22-graph merged store with 328.1M nodes and 1.44B edges. It also introduces schema-driven Model Context Protocol (MCP) server generation, achieving 98% accuracy on the BiomedQA benchmark compared to 85% for text-to-Cypher and 75% for standalone GPT-4o. This work demonstrates a scalable, reproducible approach to integrating fragmented biomedical data, which could significantly accelerate drug discovery and clinical research by providing unified access to knowledge across siloed databases. The MCP server generation also shows how AI agents can reliably query complex knowledge graphs, bridging the gap between LLMs and structured biomedical data. The system loads all 24 snapshots in 2,269.1 seconds with a peak RSS of 366.5 GB (254 bytes per edge), merging 132,511 cross-source entities across five audited keys with zero conflicting-ID refusals. On a frozen 81-query suite, 62 queries return results (17 data-gap empties, 2 timeouts), and on an expanded 200-query oracle suite, 195 pass (97.5%).
