## [ES2 fine-tuning separates harmful and harmless embeddings to boost LLM safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.20206) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes Embedding Space Separation (ES2), a fine-tuning method that increases the distance between harmful and harmless embeddings to make adversarial attacks harder. It uses an embedding anchor loss and a KL-divergence loss to preserve model capabilities, and experiments show strong defense against various attacks with general performance comparable to baselines. Safety alignment alone does not prevent LLMs from producing harmful content under adversarial attacks, so ES2 offers a practical defense that raises the cost of embedding-space manipulation. This could benefit researchers and practitioners working on AI safety and robustness, especially those deploying aligned models in adversarial settings. Maximizing embedding separation alone can damage LLM capabilities, so ES2 adds an embedding anchor loss that keeps harmless embeddings directionally close to a frozen base model and a KL-divergence loss that keeps output distributions on harmless prompts close to the base model. Some performance degradation is still observed on certain tasks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: LLMs are typically safety-aligned via fine-tuning to refuse harmful requests, but adversarial attacks can still bypass these safeguards by subtly perturbing inputs in embedding space. Prior work observes that such attacks often shift harmful embeddings toward harmless regions, motivating defenses that increase separation between the two. KL-divergence regularization is commonly used in fine-tuning to keep a model's outputs close to a reference model and avoid degrading general abilities.

## [STRIATUM-CTF: MCP-Based Agent Framework for Autonomous CTF Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22577) ⭐️ 7.0/10

STRIATUM-CTF is a modular agentic framework that uses the Model Context Protocol (MCP) to mediate between LLM reasoning and offensive-security tools, enforcing schema validation to reject malformed tool calls before execution. In a controlled study of 180 runs across 15 CTF challenges, it achieved a 77.8–86.7% success rate, and it placed first in a live multi-team CTF at Florida State University against over 30 teams. This work demonstrates a practical approach to making LLM agents more robust in high-stakes cybersecurity tasks by constraining tool use through a standardized protocol, which could influence the design of future autonomous cyber operation systems. It also adds to the growing evidence that LLM-based agents can compete in CTF competitions, fueling debate about the future of such contests. The controlled study isolates documentation volume rather than the protocol layer itself, since all four ablation conditions share the same tool layer; the live CTF win was a single non-replicated event decided by a 10-point margin, so the authors present schema-mediated grounding as a design principle with qualitative support rather than a measured effect.

Capture-the-Flag (CTF) competitions are cybersecurity challenges where participants solve security puzzles, often used for training and recruitment. LLM agent orchestration involves coordinating a language model with various tools and APIs to perform complex tasks, but naive agents that directly execute model output can be brittle due to malformed commands and context loss.

## [Humans Cannot Reliably Detect LLM-Generated News, Large Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03755) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A study using the JudgeGPT platform collected 2,318 judgments from 1,054 participants across news articles generated by six LLMs and found that participants could not reliably distinguish machine-generated from human-written text (p > .05, Welch's t-test). The inability held across all models, including open-weight models with as few as 7B parameters, and accuracy degraded after approximately 30 sequential evaluations due to cognitive fatigue. The findings suggest that user-side detection of AI-generated text is not a viable defense against misinformation, motivating system-level countermeasures such as cryptographic content provenance. This has implications for platform design, media literacy, and policy discussions around AI transparency. Self-reported domain expertise predicted judgment accuracy (r = .35, p < .001) whereas political orientation did not (r = -.10, n.s.), and clustering revealed distinct response strategies labeled "Skeptics" versus "Believers". The dual-axis design independently measured source attribution (human vs. machine) and authenticity judgment (legitimate vs. fake) on continuous scales.

## [Mstar Evolves Task-Specific Memory Programs for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11811) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Mstar introduces a method that automatically discovers task-optimized memory programs for LLM agents through reflective code evolution, representing each memory harness as an executable Python program with Schema, Logic, and Instruction components that are jointly optimized. Evaluated on four tasks spanning conversation, embodied planning, and specialized reasoning, Mstar outperforms existing baselines across all tasks. This work challenges the one-size-fits-all approach to agent memory by showing that different domains benefit from structurally distinct memory harnesses, which could lead to more adaptive and efficient LLM agent architectures. It also demonstrates that program search is a viable mechanism for automatically tailoring agent infrastructure to specific tasks. The evolved memory programs exhibit structurally distinct processing mechanisms for each evaluated domain, confirming that task-specific memory designs emerge from the search process. As an arXiv preprint, the work lacks peer review and community discussion, and the reported gains are described as incremental rather than paradigm-shifting.

Agent harnesses manage tool use, memory, state persistence, and feedback loops around the model, and recent work emphasizes that memory is tightly coupled to the harness. Reflective code evolution is an LLM-guided evolutionary approach where a model iteratively proposes, evaluates, and refines code to optimize a target objective.

## [Survey Maps Security Risks of Persistent Memory in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16548) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv survey (2604.16548v5) proposes a Memory Lifecycle Framework that organizes attacks, defenses, and cross-phase dependencies across six lifecycle phases (Write, Store, Retrieve, Execute, Share & Propagate, Forget & Rollback) and four security objectives (Integrity, Confidentiality, Availability, Governance). It also introduces Verifiable Memory Governance (VMG), a set of five architectural primitives for auditable, recoverable control over agent memory state. Persistent, writable memory fundamentally changes the threat model for LLM agents, enabling cross-session poisoning, unauthorized access, and propagation of malicious state across shared organizational contexts. This survey provides researchers and practitioners with a structured vocabulary and governance blueprint for securing agent memory, an area that existing input-centric security approaches do not adequately address. The VMG framework formalizes five primitives—Write Authorization, Provenance Visibility, Principal-Scoped Retrieval, Rollbackability, and Verified Forgetting—each with a predicate definition and evaluation metric. The survey argues for integrating provenance, versioning, and policy-aware retention with retrieval and execution defenses to support auditable prevention, containment, and recovery across the lifecycle.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: LLM agents increasingly use writable, cross-session persistent memory to remember user preferences and past interactions, moving beyond stateless prompt-response patterns. Unlike prompt injection, which affects a single input, memory poisoning can persist and propagate across sessions, making it a qualitatively different and harder-to-detect security challenge. Prior work has examined memory architectures and specific attacks, but this survey is among the first to systematically map the full memory lifecycle and propose governance-level requirements.

The paper introduces 3D-ARD+, a 3D Autoregressive Diffusion model that sequentially generates 3D scenes from text by unifying autoregressive generation over a multimodal token sequence with diffusion generation of next-object 3D latents. It uses a two-step process: first generating coarse scene-space latents conditioned on text and existing scene, then refining object-space latents for fine geometry and appearance, trained on a curated dataset of 230K indoor scenes. This work addresses a key gap in text-to-scene generation by jointly generating layout and objects in a consistent manner, enabling more detailed and semantically aligned 3D scenes from complex text descriptions. It could benefit applications in virtual reality, gaming, and interior design by reducing manual effort and improving consistency. The model is evaluated at 7B parameters and demonstrates the ability to generate and place objects following non-trivial spatial layouts and semantics from text instructions. The two-step latent generation allows for coarse scene planning and fine object detail, though the approach relies on a large curated dataset of 230K indoor scenes.

## [Tsallis q-logarithm loss family unifies SFT and RLVR for reasoning models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25907) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a loss family J_Q based on the Tsallis q-logarithm that interpolates between RLVR (q=0) and log-marginal-likelihood over latent trajectories (q=1), with the standard SFT→RLVR pipeline corresponding to a stepwise q=1→q=0 schedule. It also derives two Monte Carlo estimators, GARL and PAFT, that directly optimize fixed-q without annotated rationales or external verifiers, and shows GARL at high q escapes cold start on FinQA, HotPotQA, and MuSiQue where GRPO fails under Qwen 3 and Gemma 4 architectures. This framework could enable training of reasoning models in regimes where the standard two-step pipeline fails—when annotated rationales are unavailable, too weak, or when the model is too unaligned for RLVR cold start. By unifying supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards under one continuum, it offers a principled way to trade off exploration and memorization, potentially broadening RLVR beyond math and coding to less structured domains. Gradient flow analysis shows the exploitation pole (q=0) needs Ω(1/p0) time to escape cold start but is robust to label noise, while the density-estimation pole (q=1) escapes in Θ(log(1/p0)) but memorizes label noise, explaining why SFT precedes RLVR. Both GARL and PAFT share bias O(q/(M P_θ^q)) but differ in variance and stability, and all members of J_Q share the same gradient direction, differing only by a per-instance amplification P_θ^{-q}.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Training reasoning models typically uses supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on annotated rationales followed by reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), where a rule-based checker provides the reward. RLVR works well for tasks with checkable answers like math and coding but struggles when no verifiable reward exists or when the model is too unaligned to start. The Tsallis q-logarithm is a generalization of the standard logarithm from Tsallis statistics that deforms how probabilities are weighted, and the log-marginal-likelihood over latent trajectories is a density-estimation objective that integrates over possible reasoning paths.

EvoMemBench introduces a unified benchmark for LLM agent memory organized along two axes: memory scope (in-episode vs. cross-episode) and memory content (knowledge-oriented vs. execution-oriented). The study compares 15 representative memory methods with strong long-context baselines under a standardized protocol, revealing that current memory systems are not yet a general solution. This benchmark addresses a significant gap in agent evaluation by providing a systematic way to assess memory mechanisms, which are essential for agents to store, update, and retrieve information over time. The findings that long-context baselines remain highly competitive and no single memory form works consistently across all settings will inform future research and development of more effective memory systems for LLM-based agents. The benchmark reveals that memory helps most when the current context is insufficient or tasks are difficult, retrieval-based methods remain strong for knowledge-intensive settings, and procedural and long-term memory methods are more effective for execution-oriented tasks when their stored experience matches the task structure. The dataset and code are available at https://github.com/DSAIL-Memory/EvoMemBench.

Experiments on three lightweight VLMs show substantial gains on DRBench while preserving or improving general-purpose benchmark performance, with Qwen2.5-VL-3B trained via DRScaffold surpassing the frozen Qwen2.5-VL-32B under zero-shot direct-answer prompting on DRBench. ## [Relative-Computability Theory for Self-Improving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27381) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces a relative-computability framework for self-improving agents, adding explicit acquisition and completion contracts to an oracle-relative model to separate learning a solver from certifying its independence from a teacher. It proves a central obstruction: no baseline-effective procedure can uniformly extract a valid completion specification from arbitrary total programs, even in one feature dimension or with two candidate descriptions. The work provides a formal foundation for understanding when an AI agent can safely stop relying on its teacher, which is crucial for AI safety and the design of autonomous self-improving systems. It clarifies the information-theoretic limits of certifying independence, potentially guiding more reliable agent architectures. The framework uses a guarded protocol that allows query-proposer revision while guaranteeing completion with the optimal number of teacher calls, and a supplied separating specification extends it to finite families. Controlled experiments distinguish exact teacher removal from low-risk stopping and measure adaptation benefits and costs against a stronger greedy baseline.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Relative computability, introduced by Turing in 1939, generalizes Turing computability using oracle Turing machines that can query an external oracle. Self-improving agents are AI systems that autonomously refine their own capabilities, often learning from a teacher or oracle. This paper combines these concepts to formally study the process of an agent acquiring a capability and then certifying it no longer needs the teacher.

## [Optimal Transport Unifies SAE Feature Matching and Circuit Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28567) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces a distributional framework that represents each sparse autoencoder (SAE) feature as an activation-weighted distribution over hidden states, rather than a single decoder vector. By projecting these distributions into a shared reference space and comparing them with Wasserstein distance, the method provides a unified semantic metric for cross-layer feature matching and automatic compression of large feature circuits into interpretable supernodes. This work addresses two key SAE analyses that have been difficult to scale—cross-layer feature matching and circuit compression—by showing they are instances of the same underlying problem. A unified, theoretically grounded metric could make SAE-based mechanistic interpretability more scalable and reliable, benefiting researchers working on understanding and compressing large language models. The authors prove that their representation is invariant to activation rescaling, stable under perturbations, and recovers true matches under finite-sample margin conditions. Empirically, the method outperforms decoder-vector and LLM-based baselines and captures subtle functional distinctions between related features, while automatically compressing large feature circuits into interpretable supernodes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are a central tool in mechanistic interpretability: they decompose language model activations into sparse, potentially interpretable features. However, features learned by SAEs can be inconsistent across layers and training runs, making it hard to match semantically similar features and to compress large circuits—subgraphs performing specific functions—into understandable units. Optimal transport, and specifically the Wasserstein distance, provides a principled way to measure the distance between probability distributions, which the paper leverages to compare feature representations.

## [Probing Video Foundation Models for Intuitive Physics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09646) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2606.09646v3) systematically probes frozen representations of three video foundation models—V-JEPA (predictive joint-embedding), VideoMAE (masked reconstruction), and LTX-Video (diffusion generator)—on the IntPhys2 and Minimal Video Pairs (MVP) benchmarks to test whether intuitive-physics information is encoded. It finds physics-relevant signal is weakest in early layers and most accessible at intermediate-to-late depth, with temporal attentive probes substantially outperforming linear readouts on MVP (V-JEPA reaching 95.0% pair consistency) while VideoMAE achieves the strongest IntPhys2 attentive result at 73.9% violation-of-expectation accuracy. This work provides empirical evidence that intuitive physics is broadly decodable from video foundation models but is not a single uniform capability, challenging assumptions that a simple hierarchy of pretraining objectives or model scale determines physical understanding. It matters for AI/ML researchers designing video models and for downstream applications like robotics and world models, where reliable physical reasoning is critical. The study compares linear, MLP, and temporal attentive probes across layers and backbone variants, finding that accessibility of physics information depends more on the benchmark and readout type than on pretraining paradigm or scale. Notably, attentive probe performance on LTX-Video consistently underperforms V-JEPA and VideoMAE on both benchmarks, and linear probes recover more signal on IntPhys2 than on MVP.

**Background**: Video foundation models are large neural networks pretrained on massive video data to learn general-purpose representations. The paper introduces BeyondArena, the first unified holistic benchmark for tabular foundation models that supports diverse task types (IID, temporal, grouped) across sample size and feature dimensionality scales, along with DataFoundry, a Python framework and metadata schema for curating tabular datasets. The benchmark covers IID, temporal, and grouped tasks, includes diverse feature types such as text and high cardinality, and spans a broad range of disciplines. ## [Copula-Theoretic Benchmark Suite for Mutual Information Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03487) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes a unified copula-theoretic benchmarking framework for mutual information (MI) estimation, with two test families: a copula-first family that varies ground-truth MI, dimensionality, and marginal complexity using synthetic and flow-based transformations, and a marginals-first family that couples real-world image data with controlled dependency structures. It evaluates non-parametric, variational, and generative estimators, finding no universal winner, and releases open-source code. Existing MI benchmarks rely on simplified low-dimensional distributions, so this framework fills a gap by enabling evaluation on complex, realistic data and revealing that each estimator class can outperform others in specific setups. This could guide method selection and inspire new estimators that address identified fundamental barriers. The framework subsumes existing benchmarks as special cases and uses flow-based transformations to control marginal complexity. The evaluation shows that non-parametric, variational, and generative estimators each have regimes where they systematically outperform all others, and the authors propose new tests that stress specific limitations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Mutual information measures the dependence between random variables and is central to machine learning and statistics, but it lacks closed-form expressions for complex distributions, necessitating estimators. Copula theory separates a joint distribution into marginal distributions and a copula that captures dependence, and mutual information equals the negative copula entropy, providing a unified view. Non-parametric estimators (e.g., k-NN based), variational estimators (neural bounds like MINE), and generative estimators (e.g., using normalizing flows) are three main classes, each with known bias-variance trade-offs.

## [Free Energy Landscape of Dense Associative Memory Derived via Large Deviations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19195) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper uses large deviations theory to derive a general free energy functional for a broad class of associative memories, including dense associative memories. It reproduces classical Hopfield results, derives temperature-dependent free energy for dense memories with polynomial interactions and Log-Sum-Exponential (LSE) activation, and obtains the disorder-averaged ground-state energy landscape in the extensive limit. This provides a systematic analytical framework for understanding memory retrieval in complex associative memory architectures, which could impact the design and theoretical understanding of modern Hopfield networks and their connections to deep learning. Researchers in statistical physics of neural networks and machine learning theory will benefit from the exact thresholds and energy landscape results. The method yields the exact error-free threshold for the LSE model and reveals how memory retrieval depends on the initial state in higher-order dense networks. The analysis is for a finite number of patterns and considers the extensive limit for the ground-state energy landscape.

**Background**: Dense associative memories (also known as modern Hopfield networks) are generalizations of classical Hopfield networks that can store many more patterns than the number of neurons. Large deviations theory is a branch of probability theory that deals with rare events and provides tools for analyzing exponential decay of probabilities. The Log-Sum-Exponential (LSE) function is a smooth approximation to the maximum function, often used as an activation function in neural networks.

## [CogBattery benchmarks 54 LLMs against human cognitive data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24999) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CogBattery evaluates 54 open-weight language models on text adaptations of 13 cognitive paradigms, comparing each to human data from the original studies. It finds that models replicate the direction of human effects (e.g., more false alarms on n-back lures than controls) but diverge in error patterns, confidence calibration, and lack scale-consistent human-likeness. This work shows that matching human effect directions is insufficient to establish human-like behavior, challenging common assumptions in cognitive-science-inspired LLM evaluation. It provides condition-level human references that can be used to test error patterns and confidence reports in new models, relevant to both AI evaluation and cognitive science. Most models' reported confidence separates correct from incorrect answers less well than humans', and most always choose the same wager even when they classify accurately. Model scores share two dimensions, the first closely tied to scale, yet larger models are not consistently closer to humans, and predefined cognitive groupings show no reliable predictive advantage over random groupings of the same size.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Cognitive tasks like the n-back task measure working memory by asking participants to detect when a stimulus repeats after n steps; lures are similar but non-matching stimuli that elicit false alarms. Confidence calibration refers to how well a model's stated confidence matches its actual accuracy. Open-weight models are those whose parameters are publicly released, allowing researchers to run and study them directly.

## [ReBRAC-v2 modernizes behavior-regularized actor-critic with normalizing flows, outperforming offline RL baselines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01205) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ReBRAC-v2 introduces a modernized behavior-regularized actor-critic that trains an exact-likelihood normalizing-flow actor with likelihood, MSE, and MAE behavior regularization, classification-based residual critics, staged optimization, and multi-sample inference. It achieves an average score of 74.8 on ten OGBench categories versus 52.3 for the next-best method, and also leads on D4RL AntMaze (90.2) and Adroit (33.6). This work shows that systematic engineering of a conventional offline RL foundation can surpass expressive generative-policy methods, offering a strong reference baseline for future research. It also provides the first complete evaluation across all 235 state-based single-task problems in the original OGBench suite, which will help standardize comparisons. The method uses one shared configuration developed through roughly 600 Bayesian proposals on six OGBench tasks, with only two behavior-regularization coefficients adapted via a 16-point grid. Ablations show the complete cloning objective retains the highest average after coefficient retuning, and further analyses characterize training, capacity, and inference choices.

**Background**: Offline reinforcement learning aims to learn policies from fixed datasets without online interaction, but faces challenges like distribution shift. Behavior-regularized actor-critic methods constrain the learned policy to stay close to the dataset's behavior policy, while normalizing flows are expressive generative models that provide exact likelihoods. OGBench, D4RL AntMaze, and Adroit are standard benchmarks for evaluating offline RL algorithms.

## [GUI-Lens Enables Precise GUI Grounding for Off-the-Shelf VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03270) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GUI-Lens introduces a coarse-to-fine cropping framework that lets off-the-shelf vision-language models iteratively select regions and scales using OCR and UI component references to achieve more precise GUI grounding. Experiments on five benchmarks and six base models show consistent accuracy improvements, achieving up to 9.3 times the accuracy of direct grounding on ScreenSpot-Pro. This work addresses a key bottleneck in GUI agents: accurately mapping natural-language instructions to click locations on high-resolution, densely populated interfaces. By improving grounding without retraining, it could make existing general-purpose VLMs more reliable for automation tasks, benefiting developers building GUI agents across mobile, web, and desktop platforms. The framework extracts OCR text and detected UI components from screenshots as coordinate references, then uses the instruction and current view to select the next region and scale, iteratively cropping and enlarging until the target is determined. Proposed crops and clicks are validated against the instruction throughout, and the final local position is mapped back to original screen coordinates.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: GUI grounding is the task of mapping natural-language instructions to click locations on a screen, essential for reliable GUI agents. Vision-language models (VLMs) often recognize a requested control but fail to locate it precisely enough for interaction, especially on high-resolution, densely populated interfaces. Most existing methods rely on direct click prediction, allowing visual ambiguity or inaccurate initial estimates to propagate to the final result. GUI-Lens instead uses active visual observations through a coarse-to-fine cropping process.

## [SUDO: Simulation-free unbalanced optimal transport with general growth penalties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04710) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces SUDO, a simulation-free framework for unbalanced dynamic optimal transport (UDOT) that supports general non-quadratic convex growth penalties. It first shows that concave growth penalties lead to degenerate solutions separating growth from transport, then learns conditional paths and transport costs to solve the induced semi-coupling problem via unbalanced flow matching. Existing UDOT solvers either rely on expensive NeuralODE simulations or are limited to quadratic penalties with analytical Wasserstein-Fisher-Rao geodesics. SUDO removes both bottlenecks, enabling efficient inference of cellular dynamics from unpaired single-cell snapshots and allowing asymmetric penalties that encode proliferation-dominant biological priors. On WFR benchmarks, SUDO matches the accuracy of analytical solution-driven algorithms while being faster than simulation-based methods. Beyond WFR, it supports asymmetric penalties that yield more plausible trajectories and growth estimates on synthetic and single-cell datasets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Unbalanced dynamic optimal transport extends classical optimal transport by allowing mass to be created or destroyed along transport paths, which is essential for modeling cell proliferation and death. The growth penalty controls how growth is distributed along paths; the quadratic penalty yields the Wasserstein-Fisher-Rao (WFR) distance, for which closed-form geodesics exist. NeuralODE-based solvers simulate the transport ODE during training, which is computationally costly, while analytical solvers only handle the quadratic case.

## [TamilEOT Releases First Open Dataset and Lightweight Detectors for Tamil End-of-Turn Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05631) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TamilEOT introduces the first open dataset and detectors for semantic end-of-turn detection in a South Indian language, comprising 18,485 labelled turn boundaries from 116 real Tamil telephone conversations and two audio-only detectors built on Smart Turn v3's architecture. On a held-out split of 4,168 clips from 30 unseen calls, accuracy improves from 70.30% zero-shot to 83.71% (8.7 MB model) and 86.13% (21 MB model), with ROC-AUC rising from 0.751 to 0.921, while both models run in under 150 ms single-threaded on a laptop CPU. This work addresses a core UX problem in voice agents—deciding whether a speaker has finished or is still thinking—for Tamil, a low-resource language that lacks such resources, and shows that small, CPU-friendly models can substantially outperform zero-shot baselines. It also provides a candid account of dataset construction costs and negative results, which is valuable for practitioners building turn-detection systems in other under-resourced languages. The authors report that rule-derived negative labels scored only 44.4% against a blind human pass (below chance) and were replaced by an audio-LLM labeller at 97.5% agreement for US$5.69; among six training levers, only encoder capacity moved results, with three identical runs spanning 0.87 accuracy points. Replaying labelled boundaries through the production VAD and streaming adapter costs a further 2.60 points, and 7.8% of boundaries never reach the model at all.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Semantic end-of-turn detection is the task of deciding, at each pause in speech, whether a speaker has finished their turn or is merely pausing, using linguistic and prosodic cues rather than a fixed silence timeout. Smart Turn v3 is an open-source semantic voice activity detection model from the Pipecat project that analyzes raw audio waveforms with a Whisper Tiny encoder and a shallow linear head to predict turn completion. Zero-shot baselines apply a model trained on other data without task-specific fine-tuning, and low-resource languages like Tamil often lack the annotated corpora needed to train such detectors.

## [Coding Agents Learn and Transfer Closed-Loop Robot Policies via Software Archive](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19906) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19906v3) shows that a coding agent can learn closed-loop robot policies from a few demonstrations and simulation feedback, raising success on four RoboCasa source tasks from 28.3% to 64.2%. When the validation-selected implementations are stored in a software archive and reused for nine new target tasks, success reaches 57.0%, compared with 45.2% without references and 41.5% using initially generated source code. This reframes improved robot control code as reusable 'execution experience,' suggesting that coding agents can accumulate transferable software knowledge rather than relearning each task from scratch. If it generalizes, it could lower the cost of deploying manipulation policies and connect coding-agent research with transfer learning in robotics. The approach relies on simulation feedback for iterative code improvement and validation-based selection, and it was also validated on a physical Unitree G1 humanoid. The transfer gains depend on using validation-selected code rather than initially generated code, and the evaluation is limited to RoboCasa tasks, so real-world generality remains to be established.

Transfer learning in robotics aims to reuse prior experience to avoid learning from scratch, and this paper tests that idea at the level of complete software implementations.

## [Fairness Reframed as Distributional Stability with SOCP Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37061) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.37061v2) reframes algorithmic fairness as the stability of a predictor's outputs under perturbations to the composition of protected groups, showing that several classical fairness notions arise as special cases where the unfairness gap equals a Lipschitz constant of a prediction-rate functional. The authors propose a learning procedure based on convex combinations of reweighted predictors, formulated as a second-order cone program (SOCP), and establish generalization bounds that hold uniformly across demographic compositions without knowing the deployment distribution. This work offers a unified theoretical lens that connects disparate fairness definitions and provides deployment-agnostic guarantees, which could be valuable for practitioners who cannot know the demographic mix at test time. It also introduces a tractable convex optimization approach (SOCP) with generalization bounds, potentially making fair learning more practical and theoretically grounded. The unfairness gap is characterized as a Lipschitz constant of a prediction-rate functional, and the learning procedure solves an SOCP to find convex combinations of reweighted predictors. The guarantees are uniform over a range of demographic compositions, but the approach is an arXiv preprint and its empirical evaluation is limited to standard benchmarks, with practical impact yet to be demonstrated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Algorithmic fairness research often evaluates predictors under a fixed data distribution, but real-world deployment may involve different demographic compositions. Distributional robustness studies how predictions change under distributional shifts, and Lipschitz continuity quantifies how much a function can change relative to its input. A second-order cone program (SOCP) is a class of convex optimization problems with constraints of the form ||Ax+b||₂ ≤ cᵀx+d, which can be solved efficiently by interior-point methods and is more tractable than semidefinite programming.

## [SAE-Based Defense Suppresses Adversarial Patch Features in VLA Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03498) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.03498v2) uses a sparse autoencoder to identify a feature in Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models whose activation strongly correlates with adversarial patches, then suppresses that feature at inference time only when a linear probe detects an attack. Evaluated on LIBERO-10, this conditional intervention improves success rates under intermittent attacks without fine-tuning the VLA. This work bridges mechanistic interpretability and adversarial robustness for robotics, showing that internal attack-related representations can serve as precise defense targets. It offers a fine-tuning-free defense that could make VLA-controlled robots more reliable in the presence of physical adversarial patches, an important safety concern for real-world deployment. The defense is conditional: continuously suppressing the identified feature substantially degrades nominal policy performance, so a linear probe gates the intervention to attack periods only. The evaluation is limited to the LIBERO-10 benchmark, and the paper is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

Adversarial patches are physical or digital patterns placed in a camera's view that can confuse models into misclassification or failure. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are a mechanistic interpretability tool that decomposes neural network activations into sparse, interpretable features, and have recently been used to understand and steer large models.

## [Audit Finds Harness Gains on Longevity LLM Are Mostly Artifacts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05533) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Three preregistered studies audit a 'rescued' harness around a frozen longevity-tuned 1.7B model and find that its reported gains largely stem from unparsed rows and reference answers leaked as assistant turns, not genuine harness improvement. At 14B, two GEPA harnesses beat thinking-off by 34 points but were not detectably above the strongest constant-label baseline. This work exposes common evaluation artifacts—unparsed rows, leaked reference answers, and replication failures—that can inflate harness gains, and shows that such gains may not exceed a constant-label baseline. It serves as a methodological caution for AI/ML evaluation, especially for agentic harnesses and benchmark pipelines. The audit found that thinking-off raised test accuracy over a capped thinking setting in 7 of 12 cells, mostly on rows the cap left unparsed, and replication reproduced an injected defect's distortion in 9 of 20 evaluable cases. On prediction tasks like 14B mortality and Autocast, harnesses gave one label in 93.1% to 98.2% of parsed answers, ending near the constant baseline despite large apparent gains over thinking-off.

**Background**: A harness is the runtime scaffolding that drives model calls, manages context, and parses outputs during evaluation. The study uses a longevity-tuned 1.7B model (L-Qwen3-1.7B) and GEPA harnesses, which are optimized prompt-and-parsing pipelines, to test whether harness gains are real or artifacts of evaluation design.

## [SYNTRA: Self-improving data synthesis scales embedding-model adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14730) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SYNTRA is a self-improving agentic framework that converts a few retrieval examples into reusable synthesis instructions for generating embedding-model training data. Experiments across 24 datasets and 15 domains show it benefits strong embedding models with and without existing labelled data, and self-improvement outperforms human calibration on 4 of 6 tasks. This work addresses the costly manual design of training data that constrains embedding-model adaptation, enabling scalable domain-specific retrieval with minimal human intervention. It could benefit retrieval-augmented generation and agent systems that need specialized retrieval across diverse domains. On MS MARCO, retrieval gains persist across a 32-fold range of synthetic training-data sizes, indicating the training value scales. The framework infers implicit information needs and relevance criteria from unlabelled corpora and few query-document pairs, then refines query-generation and relevance-assessment instructions via feedback on trial outputs.

I3L introduces a low-cost, open-source teleoperation interface called JoyLo+ that uses policy-tracking leader arms and touch-triggered takeover to collect whole-body corrections, combined with iterative policy refinement (four rounds of HG-DAgger). Evaluated on three robot embodiments and eight tasks, it improves autonomous success by an average of 68.3 percentage points over base policies and achieves at least 90% success on every task. By providing an integrated hardware and learning system with an open-source interface, it offers a practical path to deploy imitation learning policies in real-world mobile manipulation tasks. The method uses four rounds of HG-DAgger, outperforming demonstration-only training by 35.6 percentage points on average with the same number of training trajectories, and reduces takeover duration per episode by 72% over four rounds. The evaluation covers eight simulated and real-world tasks, including four long-horizon whole-body mobile manipulation tasks.

## [PriorityFEC: Deadline-Aware FEC for KV-Cache Handoff in Edge LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21390) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PriorityFEC introduces a utility-aware KV-cache handoff scheme that orders high-utility KV blocks earlier in the transmission stream and protects them with a feedback-adaptive Streaming Forward Erasure Correction (FEC) mechanism scheduled before the TTFT deadline. Implemented on a distributed edge LLM serving prototype with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, it outperforms compared transport schemes, especially under stringent deadlines. This work addresses a real bottleneck in edge multi-agent LLM systems: redundant prefill computation when passing context between agents. By jointly optimizing model-side KV importance and transport-side reliability, it shows a cross-layer approach that can preserve task quality under tight latency budgets and unreliable networks. The scheme prioritizes KV blocks by utility, places them earlier in the source stream, and uses receiver feedback to adaptively schedule repair packets before the TTFT deadline. Evaluation covers multiple datasets and two 7B/8B instruction-tuned models, with advantages most pronounced under stringent deadline constraints.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: In multi-agent LLM systems, an upstream agent's processed context must often be re-prefilled by downstream agents, causing redundant computation. Reusing the prefilled key-value (KV) cache avoids this but requires transferring the cache over the network before generation can start. Forward error correction (FEC) adds redundancy to recover lost packets without retransmission, which is useful when retransmission would violate latency deadlines such as time-to-first-token (TTFT).

## [DialFork: New Benchmark Targets Procedural Hallucination in Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21561) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced DialFork, the first benchmark to measure procedural hallucination in conversational agents, built by forking conformant dialogues at controlled turn positions and injecting four types of hallucination to create 36,278 dialogues across 24 tasks in 12 domains. The work also proposes dialog-flow-aware metrics, FlowKL and FlowScore, and defines three evaluation tasks for monitoring, retrieval, and real-time guardrails. This matters because conversational agents are increasingly expected to follow procedural logic in high-stakes domains, yet existing hallucination benchmarks focus almost exclusively on factual accuracy. DialFork fills a gap by providing a validated dataset and flow-aware metrics, enabling researchers and practitioners to detect when an agent's dialogue drifts off the intended process even if each utterance is fluent and factually plausible. The dataset contains 2,134 conformant dialogues and 34,144 forks, validated by 30 human annotators, and is released under open licenses on GitHub. At the dialogue level, FlowScore significantly outperforms all baselines across seven encoders, demonstrating that semantic similarity is no substitute for procedural similarity.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Large language models are often deployed as conversational agents that must follow step-by-step procedures, such as customer support workflows or troubleshooting guides. Factual hallucination—where a model states incorrect facts—has been widely studied, but procedural hallucination, where the dialogue content is plausible yet the sequence of steps is wrong, has received little attention. DialFork addresses this by treating dialogue as a process rather than a bag of utterances, and by introducing metrics that compare how dialogues unfold over time.

A new arXiv paper (2610.31306v2) defines "superintelligent software engineering" as building software with agents whose capability exceeds human engineers, and argues that governance—gates, contracts, decisions, and decay control—must scale with that capability. It presents a simple model showing that beyond a fixed review capacity, escaping changes grow linearly with throughput while the share caught by human reading falls toward zero, and introduces OSTEON, a Claude Code plugin discipline archived on Zenodo. As coding agents rapidly improve, organizations increasingly rely on human review as the last line of defense, but this paper argues that review alone cannot catch errors built on stale knowledge once agents outpace reviewers. The framework offers a concrete governance approach for teams deploying AI coding agents, with implications for correctness, safety, and enterprise adoption of agentic software engineering. An exploratory snapshot of 452 top-starred GitHub repositories found agent context files (root-level or .github) in 48.67% (95% CI 44.1–53.3), and in median files naming repository paths, 9.5%–22% of referenced paths do not resolve. The authors explicitly report no measured effect of OSTEON and mark claims about its mechanisms as opinion and design rationale.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: AI coding agents such as those built on large language models are increasingly used to write and modify code, and some are being trained to surpass human engineers. Governance in this context refers to control mechanisms like approval gates, explicit contracts, decision records, and checks for knowledge decay—the tendency of documentation and context to become outdated. The paper builds on concerns that human code review, a traditional safeguard, may not scale to superhuman agent throughput.

## [Hierarchical Cooperative GFlowNets Improve Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33968) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Hierarchical Cooperative GFlowNets (HCG), a method that dynamically partitions the search space to coordinate multiple GFlowNets, preventing them from collapsing onto the same high-reward regions. HCG imposes a hierarchy where higher-ranked models explore freely while lower-ranked models are pushed toward unexplored areas, and it includes a theoretical characterization and a normalizer-weighted aggregation rule that provably recovers the target distribution. This work addresses a key limitation of GFlowNets—ineffective exploration leading to mode collapse—by coordinating multiple samplers, which could improve performance in scientific discovery tasks like biological sequence design and combinatorial optimization. The theoretical guarantees and asynchronous training capability make it a promising direction for scaling GFlowNets to complex, high-dimensional spaces. HCG uses a shared classifier to coordinate the hierarchy, keeping GFlowNets independently parameterized and enabling asynchronous training. Experiments across multimodal distributions, sequence generation, combinatorial optimization, and biological sequence design show improved mode coverage and target approximation, with gains increasing as more coordinated samplers are added.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets) are a class of generative models that learn to sample objects from compositional spaces with probability proportional to a reward function, making them useful for scientific discovery where diverse candidates are desired. However, standard GFlowNets can suffer from mode collapse, where the sampler concentrates on a few high-reward regions and ignores others. Training multiple GFlowNets in parallel is a naive remedy, but without coordination they may all collapse onto the same regions, leaving much of the space unexplored.

## [When Trusting a Relation Hurts: Manipulation Costs and the Trust Trap in Multi-Relational Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37239) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proves that degree gating strictly beats any selection or reweighting of a relation when part of the population is isolated on that relation and the attacker cannot afford to open the gate, and that this advantage window is sharp. It also introduces a per-node exposure-per-cost metric that, computed without any attack, identifies the relation whose hardening most raises the trap threshold in 15 of 16 cases. This work provides the first sharp theoretical justification for when ignoring a relation is safer than trusting it, giving platform operators a principled, attack-free way to decide which relation to harden. It directly affects the design of robust multi-relational graph detectors used in security-sensitive domains such as fraud detection and recommendation. The analysis extends to message passing and provides supporting tools: exact worst cases for linear relational models, certified brackets for R-GCN and tree ensembles, and a finite-sample guarantee for adversarial validation. Under this guarantee, clean-trained graph detectors fall below their graph-free counterparts in 58 of 90 settings across six graphs, and the proposed selection protocol never performs significantly worse than ignoring the graph, though with at most four relations it mostly abstains.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Multi-relational graph detectors learn from clean data how much to trust each relation, but an adversary may pay different costs to manipulate different relations. Degree gating is a mechanism that controls how much information flows through a relation based on node degree, and message passing is the core operation of graph neural networks where nodes exchange information along edges. The paper asks a fundamental question: when is using a relation worse than ignoring it, given that the attacker faces manipulation costs?

## [Calibrated Distillation and Efficient Dynamic-MoE Execution for Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39876) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces calibrated multi-teacher on-policy distillation (C-MOPD) combined with efficient dynamic-MoE execution techniques for post-training dynamically routed MoE models. On Turing-20B-A2B, the approach achieves a four-domain normalized integration score of 0.9988 and retains at least 90.16% of specialist gains, while reducing consolidation step time from 398.7 to 139.3 seconds (2.86× speedup). This work addresses the challenge of integrating independently optimized specialists into a single dynamically routed MoE model without losing their individual gains, which is crucial for deploying specialized models efficiently. The techniques could benefit the LLM training and serving community by enabling more efficient consolidation and inference for MoE architectures. The calibration objective with clipping scores 0.9962 at K=32, compared to 0.9724 without calibration and 0.9834 without clipping, showing the importance of both components. The model also exceeds shared SFT and Qwen3-8B on four reasoning benchmarks with an unweighted mean of 85.92, and improves over SFT on ten of twelve broader metrics.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models activate only a subset of parameters per input, improving efficiency. Dynamic routing allows a variable number of experts per token based on confidence, adapting computation to task complexity. Multi-teacher on-policy distillation trains a student model using multiple specialized teachers on its own rollouts, combining their strengths. Turing-20B-A2B is a 20B-parameter MoE model with about 2B active parameters per token, designed for latency-sensitive applications.

Safari 27.3 introduces Adaptive Dark Mode, which automatically darkens websites that lack native dark mode support. Developers can opt out by using the CSS property `color-scheme: light only`, which Safari respects. It also gives developers a simple way to prevent unwanted darkening, potentially influencing how other browsers handle forced dark mode. The `color-scheme: light only` syntax is currently only supported by Safari, allowing developers to force light mode even when the user prefers dark. This opt-out mechanism is part of the CSS Color Adjustment Module Level 1 specification.

The release also extends embeddings support to handle different content types per binary input, which is needed for multimodal models like Gemini Embedding 2. LLM is one of the most widely used open-source tools for working with language models from the command line and Python, so these changes lower the cost of default usage and make it much easier to plug in third-party or self-hosted models that expose OpenAI-compatible APIs. The multimodal embedding support also positions the tool for the growing wave of models that embed text, images, audio, and video into a shared vector space. The release accumulates about two months of features and fixes, including contributions from 12 authors, almost all AI-assisted; Willison says he treats such PRs as bug reports with attached examples and uses models like Opus 5.5 or GPT-6 Astra to help triage them. The new default only applies if the user has not set their own default model.

**Background**: LLM is Simon Willison's command-line tool and Python library for running prompts against large language models, supporting providers such as OpenAI, Anthropic, Google Gemini, and many others, including locally run models via plugins. An OpenAI-compatible API is an HTTP interface that accepts the same requests and returns the same JSON as OpenAI's API, so clients can switch providers by changing the base URL, API key, and model name. Embeddings are vector representations of content used for search, classification, and clustering; Gemini Embedding 2 is Google's first natively multimodal embedding model, mapping text, images, video, audio, and documents into a unified embedding space.

Hugging Face published a proposal on November 3 for Model Integration Contracts (MICs), a standard way to package regression tests for the Transformers library around deliberately shrunk models instead of full checkpoints. The proposal, written by Tarek Ziadé, ships alongside the transformers-mic package, a huggingface-mic collection of published contracts, and a tmic command-line tool that drafts contracts. This standard aims to improve consistency and maintainability for model contributors by providing a uniform way to test new model integrations, which could reduce regressions and streamline the process of adding models to the widely used Transformers library. It primarily affects practitioners and contributors who work with or extend the library. A Model Integration Contract (MIC) has three parts: a shrunk 'fixture' model built from a real checkpoint's config, a runnable usage path showing something people do with the model (like generating text), and human-reviewed baseline outputs. The approach uses deliberately shrunk models instead of full checkpoints to make tests lightweight and reproducible.

A new arXiv preprint (2610.41199) introduces a framework for few-shot defect detection using two structured decision models, Jev and Clef-Flash, requiring only k=2 reference images per class. The paper also proposes an agentic approach to refine text descriptions and a calibration method to improve defect probability reliability, tested across five manufacturing case studies. This work addresses a critical bottleneck in high-yield or low-volume manufacturing where collecting even 50 defective images is impractical, potentially enabling faster and cheaper deployment of visual inspection systems. It also demonstrates that structured decision models can outperform CLIP in ultra-low-data regimes, pointing to a new direction for industrial vision beyond contrastive vision-language models. With two reference images per class, Jev achieved test accuracies of 0.75–1.00 and Clef-Flash 0.71–0.99, compared to CLIP's 0.67–0.84. The agentic text refinement improved accuracy in 6 of 8 model-application combinations, and calibration reduced the Brier score in 13 of 15 configurations; replication code, an R package called 'decisions', and a no-code web app are provided.

## [Coarse3D: Controllable Sparse Structure Generation for Image-to-3D](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41231) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Coarse3D introduces a sparse-structure generation model that produces orientation-controllable and structurally faithful voxel geometry from arbitrary unposed images. It achieves this through three designs: a first-frame anchored canonical space, a dual-tower DiT, and spatial-prior guided attention. This work addresses two key limitations of existing coarse generators—uncontrollable orientation and weak 2D-3D grounding—and can directly replace the coarse stage of existing coarse-to-fine 3D pipelines. Experiments show it substantially improves sparse structure quality and consistently enhances final 3D generation, achieving state-of-the-art performance. The first-frame anchored canonical space aligns generation with the first input view while preserving object-centric normalization, and the dual-tower DiT couples generation with auxiliary feed-forward reconstruction to learn spatially grounded features. Spatial-prior guided attention further regularizes cross-attention using pixel-to-voxel correspondence.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Coarse-to-fine 3D-native generation typically relies on a first-stage sparse structure that determines global orientation and overall geometry for later refinement. Diffusion Transformers (DiT) are diffusion models that use a transformer backbone instead of a U-Net, operating on latent patches. Unposed images are images without known camera poses, which makes 3D reconstruction more challenging because the model must infer spatial relationships without explicit camera parameters.

This arXiv review surveys pinching antenna systems (PASS) for integrated sensing and communication (ISAC) in 6G, comparing single-waveguide, multi-waveguide, and segmented-waveguide architectures. It includes a segmented-waveguide case study (SWAN) that uses segment-hysteresis reinforcement learning for joint segment selection, antenna positioning, and transmit beamforming. This review provides a structured taxonomy and identifies open research directions, which could guide future work on flexible antenna architectures for ISAC. The case study shows that waveguide loss and per-segment aperture gain create tradeoffs between communication and sensing performance. The review also highlights research directions in joint transmit-receive operation, measurement-calibrated digital twins, and trustworthy control.

## [Bio-inspired signed-dyadic coding cuts SRAM reads by a third for Mandarin keyword spotting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41381) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces Predictive Signed-Dyadic Coding (PG), a bio-inspired event-based input interface that transmits only prediction-error events with power-of-two magnitudes. In Mandarin keyword spotting, INPUT-PG achieves 90.00% accuracy while reducing mean SRAM reads by 33.87% and inference cycles by 32.77%. This work shows that predictive coding principles from neuroscience can be translated into a practical hardware interface for low-power continuous speech processing, potentially enabling always-on keyword spotting in edge devices with reduced memory and compute demands. PG maintains a local prediction for each input channel and emits an event only when the residual exceeds a learned threshold; each event carries a sign and a finite power-of-two magnitude, which the receiver reconstructs using fixed-point shifts and additions. The reported gains are relative to a dense input interface baseline, and the evaluation is limited to Mandarin keyword spotting.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Predictive coding is a neuroscience-inspired framework in which a system continuously predicts incoming sensory input and only transmits deviations (prediction errors), reducing redundant data. Event-based processing, common in neuromorphic computing, transmits information only when a significant change occurs, enabling sparse and energy-efficient computation. Keyword spotting is a always-on task in voice assistants and edge devices, where low power consumption is critical.

## [ProSearch: New Benchmark for Multi-Constraint Protocol Retrieval in Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41396) ⭐️ 6.0/10

ProSearch introduces a benchmark of 1,515 queries across six experimental science domains for evaluating multi-constraint protocol retrieval, with realistic queries, 60 interdependent fields, and 3-5 explicit constraints per query. The best of 19 tested retrievers achieves only 0.290 average nDCG@10, improving to 0.474 with reranking. This benchmark fills a gap in AI for Science by systematically evaluating retrieval systems on realistic, constraint-heavy protocol queries, which are essential for experimental design and method reproduction. It provides a diagnostic testbed that could drive improvements in scientific retrieval systems and highlight the limitations of current models. The benchmark includes numerical and negation constraints, and structured analysis shows that explicit constraint matching is a strong ranking signal, while manual error analysis identifies joint constraint binding as a key bottleneck. The construction pipeline involves LLM-based extraction, data-driven constraint selection, controlled query generation, and evidence-based relevance validation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: In experimental science, researchers often need to find protocols that satisfy multiple interdependent requirements, such as specific equipment, reagents, and numerical ranges. Existing retrieval benchmarks typically evaluate queries with a single relevance judgment and lack systematic evaluation of multi-constraint retrieval. ProSearch addresses this by creating realistic queries with explicit constraints, including negation (e.g., 'no X'), to test how well retrieval systems can handle complex scientific information needs.

## [Mechanistic Study Separates Representation and Readout of Membership Signals in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41445) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces three complementary metrics—Decodable Information Score, Used Information Score, and Readout Accessibility Gap (DUG)—to separately quantify how much membership information is encoded in internal representations versus exposed through the model's readout. It finds that membership-specific effects appear in both decodable information and readout under supervised fine-tuning (SFT), but primarily in readout under reinforcement learning (RL)-based membership inference, with activation patching localizing these effects to intermediate layers. This work provides a mechanistic decomposition of membership signals, which are often lumped together as 'memorization,' potentially enabling more precise privacy auditing and targeted defenses for LLMs. It is relevant to privacy and security researchers studying membership inference and to practitioners concerned about training data leakage. The analysis covers both SFT and RL-based membership inference methods, and extends to reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) contamination, where behavioral changes can emerge without a corresponding increase in likelihood-based membership inference signals. Activation patching is used to localize membership-specific effects to intermediate layers.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Membership inference attacks aim to determine whether a specific example was part of a model's training data, and are a key tool for assessing privacy leakage in machine learning. Mechanistic interpretability seeks to reverse-engineer the internal computations of neural networks, often using causal methods like activation patching to link internal representations to outputs. Large language models are commonly post-trained with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on instruction-response pairs or with reinforcement learning (RL) to optimize reward signals, and these different training paradigms may shape how training data is represented and exposed.

## [Dual-Budget Framework for Sparse Text Diffusion Analyzed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41487) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper formulates long-context diffusion costs as two separate budgets—attention graph edges and activated experts—and analyzes a sparse-expert configuration called DualBudget across scientific summarization, multi-hop QA, dialogue, and sentence-pair prediction. The length study shows gains over baselines up to 16K tokens but measurable degradation at longer lengths, with a 20% cost reduction relative to DiffuSeq under an unspecified timing convention. This work offers a conceptual framework that connects attention sparsity and conditional expert computation for diffusion models, which could inform efficient long-context inference design. However, the mixed ablation results and lack of strong empirical gains suggest it is an incremental analysis rather than a breakthrough. The paper specifies normalized masked attention, distinguishes denoising depth from sampling iterations, and derives graph and compute bounds. Ablations reveal mixed changes across three reported scores, and the 20% cost reduction is reported under an unspecified timing convention, with the authors explicitly separating observations from unmeasured claims about training speed, absorbing states, global coherence, and deployment memory.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Diffusion models, originally popular for image generation, are increasingly applied to text generation by iteratively denoising token sequences. Long-context diffusion is expensive because it repeatedly pays for both token interaction (attention) and feed-forward computation (experts). Sparse attention methods (local, dilated, global) and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures reduce these costs by limiting edges and activations, respectively, but their combined effect on document-level information preservation is not well understood.

## [ORION: LLM Advisor with Outcome-Driven Memory Boosts 6G Slice Admission](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41563) ⭐️ 6.0/10

ORION is a hierarchical orchestrator that combines two LLM agents for intent translation and plan generation with a cooperative multi-agent RL coordinator for cross-domain slice placement in 6G networks. Its key innovation is an outcome-driven episodic memory that feeds verified feasibility outcomes back to the planner, improving admission by 4.6–11.5 percentage points over the same planner without memory. The paper shows that no fixed planning strategy dominates across all multi-domain topologies, making the plan layer the decisive performance lever. This suggests that adaptive, memory-based planning could significantly improve resource utilization and slice admission in future 6G networks. The evaluation isolates planner quality by keeping downstream selection and execution fixed, and importance-based retention in memory accounts for most of the improvement over a FIFO store. The approach is tested on a parametrized family of multi-domain topologies, with the joint decision problem being NP-hard under continuous arrivals.

**Background**: 6G network slicing allows operators to create multiple virtual networks tailored to different services over a shared infrastructure. Deploying slices across administrative domains requires deciding which domain hosts each virtual network function and how traffic crosses domain boundaries, a complex online optimization problem. LLM agents are AI systems that use large language models to reason and act, while multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) trains multiple agents to cooperate or compete in a shared environment.

## [OT-CAL: Optimal-Transport-Guided Adaptive Sample Selection for Efficient Visual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41677) ⭐️ 6.0/10

OT-CAL introduces an adaptive data selection method that uses optimal-transport-based matching to dynamically adjust sampling preferences during training, reducing backward pass computation. Experiments on three visual learning benchmarks show that selecting 50% of each mini-batch improves performance by 1.3–2.1 points over AdaSelection while achieving 1.18–1.24 times faster training than full-data training. This work addresses a key efficiency bottleneck in deep learning training by avoiding expensive backward passes on uninformative samples, potentially lowering computational costs for visual recognition tasks. It offers a principled alternative to fixed selection rules or per-sample gradient calculations, which could be adopted in resource-constrained training scenarios. OT-CAL represents each sample with five informativeness features (including difficulty, learnability, and uncertainty) and uses a curriculum vector updated via a feedback loop to adapt selection without a manual schedule. The method is evaluated on three standard visual learning benchmarks, but as a preprint it lacks peer review and broader community validation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Deep neural networks for image recognition are typically trained with mini-batches, where a forward pass computes predictions and losses, followed by a more computationally expensive backward pass (backpropagation) to update parameters. Not all samples contribute equally to learning, so sample selection methods aim to skip backward passes on less informative samples. Optimal transport is a mathematical framework for comparing probability distributions, often used in machine learning for matching and alignment tasks.

## [Frozen Medical Segmentation Encoders Fail Together More Than Expected](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41790) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The CoFailMed study audits correlated failures across pools of frozen pretrained encoders for medical segmentation, evaluating 11 public encoders on five 2D tasks and finding that four-member pools from distinct encoders have mean effective sizes of only 1.56–2.92 at the 33rd-percentile Dice threshold. An expanded 17-encoder analysis shows that checkpoints within the same encoder family remain more correlated than cross-family pairs. This work challenges the common assumption that adding more pretrained models to an ensemble automatically increases diversity, showing that nominal pool size can substantially overstate effective independence. It suggests that medical segmentation benchmarks and ensemble designs should report absolute dependence and threshold-specific effective size alongside accuracy and model count. The audit uses frozen features with separately trained 1×1 readouts and four seeds per encoder, and estimates effective pool size from failure correlations only in pools with enough failures and passes per member. Richer readouts and fine-tuning narrow the Dice-correlation gap between pairs sharing a checkpoint or the DINOv2-B/S release and all other pairs, but dependence between different encoders can increase and fine-tuning does not consistently raise effective pool size at recomputed thresholds.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: In medical image segmentation, models are often built by attaching a lightweight readout to a frozen pretrained encoder, and ensembles combine multiple such models to improve robustness. The Dice coefficient measures overlap between predicted and ground-truth masks, and ensemble diversity—whether members make different errors—is what makes averaging multiple models beneficial. This paper introduces an audit methodology to quantify how often different encoders fail on the same cases, rather than relying on average accuracy or model count.

## [Backpropagation-free learning rules underperform on time-series classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41805) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper adapts three backpropagation-free learning rules—Direct Feedback Alignment (DFA), Direct Random Target Projection (DRTP), and NoProp—to 1D convolutional networks for univariate time-series classification and evaluates them on 112 UCR datasets. The optimized learners achieve mean F1 scores of 0.76–0.79, below ROCKET (0.83) and InceptionTime (0.84), and the authors propose an analysis framework linking performance gaps to dataset properties. This is the first systematic study of backpropagation-free learners on time-series classification, extending their evaluation beyond 2D image benchmarks. It shows that these biologically plausible, hardware-friendly rules still lag behind state-of-the-art methods, and the proposed analysis framework helps explain why, guiding future work on efficient training. Intra-class temporal variability is the dataset property most consistently associated with the performance gap (partial Spearman ρ 0.28–0.40). Replacing flatten readout with global average pooling harms DFA and DRTP more than backpropagation (difference-in-differences −0.025 and −0.055), suggesting near-random convolutional features, while NoProp tolerates pooling (DD +0.013); DRTP reduces training FLOPs by 33% but trains faster only on series longer than ~700 time steps, DFA yields no computational reduction, and NoProp cuts peak memory by 38% at the cost of 48% longer training.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Backpropagation, the standard method for training deep neural networks, suffers from update locking (layers must wait for backward passes) and the weight-transport problem (forward and backward weights must match). Backpropagation-free rules like DFA, DRTP, and NoProp relax these constraints by using random feedback or local targets, potentially enabling more efficient and biologically plausible hardware. However, these rules had been evaluated almost exclusively on 2D image benchmarks like MNIST and CIFAR-10, not on time-series data.

## [SimXAI: A Simple Drop-in Layer Boosts Conversational XAI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41856) ⭐️ 6.0/10

SimXAI introduces a lightweight LLM-centric conversational layer that replaces the separate intent recognition, dialogue management, and template-based response generation modules of GUS-style ConvXAI architectures with a single tool-calling LLM. The paper also presents an evaluation suite for single- and multi-turn parsing, faithfulness, and contextualization, and reports a user study where replacing only the conversational layer of TalkToModel improved task accuracy and reduced the number of turns. This work suggests that conversational XAI systems can be simplified by leveraging tool-calling LLMs instead of maintaining complex modular pipelines, potentially making such systems easier to adapt and more effective at handling context-dependent follow-up questions. It could influence how future ConvXAI agents are designed and evaluated, benefiting researchers and practitioners building interactive explanation interfaces. SimXAI is designed as a modular interface rather than a standalone agent, bridging access to existing XAI resources via a tool-calling LLM. The evaluation covers function-selection accuracy and faithfulness across existing and new datasets, and the user study shows improved accuracy and fewer turns when replacing only the conversational layer of TalkToModel, a representative GUS-based agent.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: Conversational explainable AI (ConvXAI) agents often follow GUS-style architectures, which use separate modules for intent recognition, dialogue management, and template-based response generation to provide faithful explanations from underlying XAI methods. However, these pipelines are hard to adapt and struggle with context-dependent follow-up questions. Tool-calling (or function-calling) LLMs can decide which external tools or APIs to invoke for a user query, enabling more flexible interactions. SimXAI leverages this capability to replace the modular pipeline with a single LLM that interfaces with existing XAI resources.

## [Hybrid SMT-NRA Solver Combines 2D Cell-Jump Local Search, MCSAT, and OpenCAD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.00557) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces a two-dimensional cell-jump move, called 2D-cell-jump, which generalizes the existing cell-jump operation in local search for SMT-NRA by enabling search over an arbitrarily selected plane rather than only along a line. It then presents an extended local search framework, 2D-LS, and a hybrid framework that integrates 2D-LS, MCSAT, and OpenCAD to improve search efficiency through information exchange. This work advances the state of the art in SMT-NRA solving by combining local search with model-based and algebraic methods, potentially leading to more efficient solvers for nonlinear real arithmetic problems that arise in formal verification and program analysis. The hybrid approach could benefit researchers and practitioners who rely on SMT solvers for handling complex nonlinear constraints. The 2D-cell-jump extends the original cell-jump by allowing updates on a plane, and the hybrid framework exchanges information among 2D-LS, MCSAT, and OpenCAD. Experimental results show improvements in local search performance, though the paper does not specify the magnitude of improvement or the benchmark set used.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 3, 05:00

**Background**: SMT-NRA is the problem of determining whether a first-order formula over nonlinear real arithmetic is satisfiable, which is fundamental in formal methods. Local search methods like LS attempt to find satisfying assignments by iteratively moving through the space, while MCSAT is a model-constructing calculus that generalizes CDCL-style reasoning to SMT. OpenCAD is an open-source implementation of cylindrical algebraic decomposition, a key algorithm for real algebraic geometry that decomposes space into cells where polynomials have constant signs.

The paper introduces a neuro-symbolic meta-policy that learns which symbolic memory heuristic to apply at each decision point in partially observable reinforcement learning, while keeping execution symbolic. The approach is evaluated on train/test room splits in RoomKG, and the best configuration uses a qualifier-aware StarE-GNN.
